Global prosodic features—including total utterance
duration, speaking rate (words per second), phone
rate (phones per second), energy mean and stan-
dard deviation, zero-crossing rate, silence ratio, pause
count (pauses ≥3 frames), maximum pause dura-
tion, voiced-pitch mean and standard deviation, and
voiced-frame ratio—are concatenated with S before
the utterance-level scoring heads, following the prac-
tice in (Li et al., 2025).
This hierarchical aggregation enables progressive
learning from local articulation patterns toward global
fluency structure.
Figure 1 illustrates the overall HiPPA architec-
ture: raw audio is encoded by WavLM, a learned
softmax-weighted combination across WavLM lay-
ers produces the acoustic stream, a cross-attention
module aligns acoustic states against reference-
conditioned phoneme embeddings, and hierarchical
aggregation feeds phoneme-level states into word-
level and sentence-level scoring heads.

Traditional pronunciation assessment systems
have largely relied on acoustic modelling techniques
based on forced alignment, Hidden Markov Models
(HMMs), and Goodness of Pronunciation (GOP)
scoring methods (Witt and Young, 2000). These ap-
proaches estimate pronunciation quality by aligning
learner speech with canonical phoneme sequences
and measuring posterior confidence scores from
acoustic models such as the TDNN-F system re-
leased with SpeechOcean762 (Zhang et al., 2021).
While these methods have shown effectiveness for
phoneme-level pronunciation scoring, they are inher-
ently limited in modelling contextual relationships re-
quired for word-level intelligibility and sentence-level
fluency assessment.
The
introduction
of
large-scale
self-
supervised
speech
encoders—most
prominently
wav2vec 2.0 (Baevski et al., 2020), HuBERT (Hsu
et al., 2021), and WavLM (Chen et al., 2022)—has
significantly

continent, while La Niña events often intensify monsoon rainfall [3]. Climate outlooks
indicate the possibility of ENSO-related anomalies during 2026, highlighting the im-
portance of improved forecasting systems.
Traditional dynamical climate models, such as General Circulation Models (GCMs),
provide seasonal ENSO forecasts but face limitations in prediction skill beyond 6–9
months due to the “spring predictability barrier” [4]. Recent advances in deep learning
have demonstrated superior forecasting skill at longer lead times (12–22 months) by ex-
tracting complex spatiotemporal patterns from vast reanalysis archives [1, 3]. These
data-driven approaches leverage Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Long
Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks to capture nonlinear dynamics that traditional
statistical methods and dynamical models struggle to represent.
1.2

The motivation for this project stems from three critical factors:

ABSTRACT
The 2026 El Niño event is forecasted with high probability, posing significant climatic
risks globally, particularly for South Asia’s monsoon-dependent agriculture and water
security. This project proposes the development of a deep learning framework that fore-
casts the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) state at lead times of 3–6 months and
translates these predictions into deterministic impact assessments for South Asian pre-
cipitation and temperature anomalies. Leveraging publicly available reanalysis datasets
(ERA5, ORAS5) and ENSO indices, the model will be trained and validated on recent
historical data (1980-2025).The proposed approach employs a Convolutional Neural
Network – Temporal Convolutional Network (CNN-TCN) hybrid architecture wherein
the CNN component captures spatial patterns (east-west SST gradients, spatial ENSO
patterns across the tropical Pacific) from each monthly snapshot and the TCN compo-

5.4
Cross-Evaluation Discussion
The two evaluations answer different questions and should
be read together. The static-k results presented above iso-
late the retrieval core on public data without engine wrap-
ping; the dynamic-k run that follows in Section 5.3 measures
the planner’s chosen retrieval in conjunction with synthe-
sis through DeepEval’s generation and retrieval-grounding
metrics. Together they cover both directions of the stan-
dard retriever-vs-engine decomposition; neither subsumes
the other, and both belong in the evaluation battery.

The principal contributions of this paper are:
1. A reference-conditioned hierarchical transformer
that explicitly aggregates phoneme representa-
tions into word and sentence heads, rather than
treating each granularity in parallel.
2. A cross-attention alignment module between
acoustic states and canonical phoneme embed-
dings,
providing a learned spoken–reference
alignment signal.

regression, phone Pearson, word/sentence regres-
sion, word-stress binary classification, and CTC.

benchmark reporting phoneme, word, and sen-
tence PCC for the full label set, alongside a dis-
cussion of the relative strengths and weaknesses
of hierarchical multi-granularity modelling com-

2. SYSTEM DESIGN
2.1 Architecture
The University Management System employs a standard 3-Tier Web Application
Architecture:
•
Presentation Tier (Client-Side): Web browsers render HTML pages (from the `templates/` directory)
styled with CSS (`static/css/`). Basic JavaScript (`static/scripts/`) handles minor client-side
interactions. Jinja2 templating dynamically generates HTML.
•
Application Tier (Server-Side): The Flask application (`main.py`) processes HTTP requests, manages
user sessions, implements business logic (validation, grading), interacts with the database (via
PyMySQL), and handles email notifications (via Flask-Mail).
•
Data Tier (Database): An externally hosted MySQL database (Aiven cloud) stores persistent data.
The schema, defined in `database_prerequisite.py`, includes tables for students, faculty, courses,
etc., with integrity enforced by constraints.

No planner; fixed-k retriever; no agent decision loop.
The planner that normally sits at the top of the LangGraph
graph and directs every query to retrieve only by con-
text is not exercised in this harness — the query is always
passed straight to dense retrieval with a fixed k, and the
cross-encoder reranker is applied on top of that, and that is
the end of the pipeline. Because the planner/retrieval agent
layer is bypassed, this evaluation breaks the SaviraNous en-
gine pipeline down to its retrieval core.

3.4.3
Feature Engineering
Input sequences are constructed consisting of 12 consecutive monthly snapshots of four
spatial fields extracted over the tropical Pacific domain (30◦S–30◦N, 120◦E–80◦W),
which represents the primary region of El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) variabil-
ity. The four spatial fields are as follows:
• Sea Surface Temperature (SST) anomaly, sourced from the European Centre
for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts Reanalysis version 5 (ERA5), representing
the primary indicator of ENSO state at the ocean surface.
• Sea Level Pressure (SLP) anomaly, sourced from ERA5, capturing the atmo-
spheric pressure patterns associated with the Walker Circulation and trade wind
variability.
• Wind stress anomaly, sourced from ERA5, representing the strength and di-
rection of trade winds along the equatorial Pacific, which drive the east-west
redistribution of warm surface water characteristic of ENSO events.

The static-k run exercises the retrieval core alone (no
planner, no council) over four public BEIR benchmarks
(n = 100 per dataset). The headline that the reranker helps
on the multi-hop set (musique nDCG@10 0.555 →0.612)
but hurts on the very-short biomedical set (scifact 0.746 →
0.730) is consistent with prior BEIR literature and is an hon-
est signal about where the retrieval core pays for itself, in-
dependent of the planner’s decisions. Static-k therefore an-
swers the question “is the retrieval core correct on public
data at fixed depths?”.

3.7
Multi-Task Prediction Heads
The model jointly optimises multiple pronunciation
scoring tasks with task-specific regression heads.
Three levels of prediction heads are learned simulta-
neously.
Phoneme-Level Head.
Predicts phone accuracy in
{0,1,2}, normalised to [0,1] for stable regression.
Word-Level Heads.
Predict word accuracy, word
stress, and word total scores.
Because word-stress
labels in SpeechOcean762 are highly imbalanced
(mostly 10 vs. a long tail of 5), the stress head is im-
plemented as a binary classification problem (correct
vs. incorrect) using BCEWithLogitsLoss with class
weighting, as recommended in (Chen et al., 2024).

print(f"Error creating tables or constraints: {err}", file=sys.stderr)
if connection:
connection.rollback()
finally:
if cursor:
cursor.close()
if connection:
connection.close()

1
Introduction
Sorting is, together with searching, the oldest and most studied problem in computer science.
Every algorithm textbook devotes a chapter to it, introduces a small set of canonical algorithms,
and ranks them by asymptotic cost: bubble, selection, and insertion sort are 𝑂(𝑛2); merge sort,
quicksort, and heap sort are 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛); counting sort is 𝑂(𝑛+ 𝑘). The implicit lesson is that
the only serious contenders are the 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) trio, and that once an algorithm is in that class the
distinction between its members is academic.
Empirically this is wrong on both halves of the lesson. The 𝑂(𝑛2) algorithms do not merely
compete on small inputs — on already-sorted inputs the fastest of them (insertion sort and bubble
sort) outperforms every 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) algorithm including C++ std::sort (0.20 ms and 0.27 ms
versus 1.25 ms at 𝑁= 105). And the 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) algorithms are not interchangeable: at 𝑁= 105

3.3
Reference-Conditioned Phoneme
Encoding
Unlike conventional pronunciation scoring systems
that process only learner audio, HiPPA explicitly in-
corporates expected pronunciation structure.
Sub-
phoneme modelling (Chao et al., 2023) has shown
that finer-than-phoneme units can further improve hi-
erarchical pronunciation assessment; HiPPA retains
the canonical phoneme as its base unit and adds
reference-conditioning rather than finer sub-phoneme
segmentation. Each canonical phoneme is embedded
using three representations:
• A learned phoneme embedding Ephone(pi).
• A positional embedding Epos(i) that disam-
biguates
multiple
occurrences
of
the
same
phoneme within an utterance.
• A six-dimensional handcrafted phonetic feature
vector Fphone(pi) ∈R6 encoding place, manner,
voicing, vowel height, vowel backness, and a
consonant–vowel binary indicator, as in classical
phonetic-feature representations.
The phoneme representation is

Introduction
1.1
Background and Motivation
Integrating large language models (LLMs) into tutoring sys-
tems has changed how educational software interacts with
users. It can chat like a real person and adjust to the way
each user asks questions. In real-world learning, an AI tutor
has to work with many types of study materials that users
bring themselves. A typical study session might involve lec-
ture slides, course syllabi, textbook chapters, handwritten
notes, recorded video transcripts, and web documentation.
To really help the user, the tutor must do two things: base

establish their background knowledge from scratch.
2. Intra-Session Context Blindness and Working-
Memory Fragmentation: During a long study ses-
sion, AI tutors often lose track of how the conversa-
tion connects to the course materials. Because systems
dump everything—chat messages, tool outputs, and
document notes—into one big pile, a laptop’s memory
fills up fast and pollutes the context. To prevent the con-
text bloating, the system usually deletes older messages
or compresses them into poor summaries and leaves the
tutor unable to follow the lesson.
3. Limited Evidence Selection in RAG Pipelines: Lan-
guage models are designed to sound convincing, but
they do not automatically select the most relevant ev-
idence from a large candidate set. Many systems in-
ject retrieved chunks into the prompt and rely on the
LLM to choose useful context. Without resource-level
routing, reranking, and confidence filtering, irrelevant
evidence can remain in the prompt and reduce answer
grounding.

3. AutoTutor – Intelligent Tutoring System
AutoTutor is an intelligent tutoring system designed to simulate the interaction
between a student and a human tutor. The system engages learners through dialogue, asking
questions and analyzing their responses to provide feedback. This interactive approach
encourages students to actively participate in the learning process.
Using natural language processing techniques, AutoTutor evaluates student
responses and provides hints or explanations when necessary. This helps students gradually
reach the correct understanding of a concept. The system focuses on improving conceptual
learning through guided conversation.

Content:
• Missing Value Handling
Missing values arising from sensor gaps or reanalysis artifacts are handled through a
three-tier strategy applied sequentially across all ingested variables.
Temporal Gap Filling: Isolated missing months (≤2 consecutive time steps) are filled
using linear interpolation between adjacent valid time steps, defined as:
X(t) = X(tbefore) +
t −tbefore
tafter −tbefore
× [X(tafter) −X(tbefore)]
(3-1)
where X(t) is the interpolated value at missing time step t, X(tbefore) is the observed
value at the last valid time step preceding the gap, X(tafter) is the observed value at
the first valid time step following the gap, and tbefore and tafter are the time indices of
the bounding observed values. This approach is physically justified as ocean and atmo-
spheric variables evolve smoothly over monthly timescales, rendering a linear estimate
between two adjacent months an appropriate approximation.

Content:
The data preprocessing pipeline (Figure 3-2) transforms raw climate reanalysis fields
into analysis-ready inputs through four sequential operations: spatial standardisation
via area-weighted block averaging to a 2◦×2◦grid, temporal alignment ensuring all
variables share the 1980–2025 monthly time axis, climatological anomaly computation
by subtracting the long-term monthly mean, and z score normalization to scale all fields
to data to have a mean of 0 and a standard deviation of 1 range for stable model training.
3.3
Data Sources
The project utilizes the following publicly available datasets:
3.3.1
Oceanic & Atmospheric Reanalysis
• ERA5 Reanalysis (ECMWF): Monthly SST, sea level pressure (SLP), 2m tem-
perature (T2M), and wind stress fields at 1°×1° resolution (1950–2025).
• ORAS5 (ECMWF): Ocean heat content and subsurface temperature profiles for
the tropical Pacific.
• GODAS (NOAA/CPC): Ocean temperature and current fields.

Content:
research investigates the wall-clock performance of seven textbook sorts and C++ std::sort
on three controlled input distributions in order to provide a clearer, quantitative understanding of
the relationship between asymptotic complexity, input distribution, and real performance.
1.3
Research Objectives
The objectives of this study are:
1. To examine the wall-clock performance of seven textbook sorting algorithms (bubble,
selection, insertion, merge, quick, heap, and counting) on random, sorted, and reverse-
sorted inputs.
2. To identify which algorithm is fastest on each input distribution and the structural reason
for that ordering.
3. To analyse the gap between textbook merge sort and quicksort and the production C++
std::sort (Introsort) on identical inputs.
4. To evaluate why major standard libraries do not ship plain merge sort or quicksort, and
what design pattern they converge on instead.
1.4
Research Questions
The research questions addressed by this study are:

Research Gap
The literature shows that new RAG research has solved spe-
cific limits of old retrieval systems. Adaptive-RAG gives
query-dependent routing.
Corrective RAG adds retrieval
verification. Self-RAG adds self-reflection. GraphRAG and
HippoRAG improve structured multi-hop reasoning. RAP-
TOR gives representations for long documents. LightRAG
focuses on efficiency.
MemoRAG focuses on memory-
guided retrieval.

reduced the number of chunks passed to the reranker in re-
source heavy sessions which kept the reranking cost close

While the systems summarized above advance retrieval ac-
curacy, they do not address the operational realities of run-
ning a personalized, multi-session tutor on local edge hard-
ware. We identify four fundamental limitations in existing
approaches:
1. Cross-Session Statelessness and Lack of User Mod-
eling: Conventional LLM-based tutoring systems op-
erate statelessly across independent sessions. The mo-
ment a session ends or the runtime process restarts, the
system flushes its active cache and discards all con-
text. Because it maintains no persistent user profile,
the system cannot track a user’s longitudinal learn-
ing curve, recurring misconceptions, or concept mas-
tery over time. Every new session effectively begins
from an uninformed cold start, forcing the user to re-
establish their background knowledge from scratch.
2. Intra-Session Context Blindness and Working-
Memory Fragmentation: During a long study ses-
sion, AI tutors often lose track of how the conversa-

4. Lack of an Integrated Architecture: Previous re-
search treats memory, document search, multi-agent
debate, and routing as separate tools rather than build-
ing them together into a single system.
Because of
this, today’s tools remain disconnected: search sys-
tems ignore a user’s past progress, memory tools choke
on large course files, and verification tools may rely
on external services that are unsuitable for privacy-
sensitive local use. What is missing is a unified system
that connects these pieces—combining long-term user
tracking, focused document searches, and evidence-
grounded retrieval while respecting the memory limits
of a standard laptop.

4.6
Speaker-Group Analysis
To analyse generalisation across different speaker
groups, performance is evaluated separately for adult
and child speech, following (Zhang et al., 2021).
Performance is consistently stronger for adult
speakers than for child speakers, suggesting greater
acoustic variability in child pronunciation patterns.
The adult/child gap motivates the speaker-group
adapter extension discussed in Section 5.

signals. The profile gets updated using EMA (Exponentials
Moving Average) so that a single noisy chat cannot snap the
learner to the extreme estimate. A default is set to cold start

the current state of learner. A separate idle trigger is invoked

asynchronously so that the user doesn’t have to wait till the
insights are generated and upserted to the profiles. Profiler
is not invoked after each turn; rather, it is triggered after a
period of inactivity in the chat without requiring explicit in-
vocation to generate and upsert the profile. Profiles are also
designed to outlive a single interaction just like the LTM.

3.9
Post-hoc Calibration
After training, a Ridge + HistGradientBoosting blend
(0.35/0.65) is fit on the training-set predictions to re-
calibrate phone scores using utterance-, word-, and
sentence-level context. Stacked generalisation (meta-
learner on model ∪Ridge ∪HGB) and per-phone iso-
tonic regression are implemented as alternative cali-
brators; the simpler Ridge+HGB blend is used for the
headline numbers reported in this paper.

3.3.4
Retriever and Reranker
The retrieval planner’s plan is executed by performing simi-
larity search over the chunk-level FAISS index, restricted to
the chunks belonging to the candidate resources. The chunks
retrieved using cosine similarity may not be hierarchically
valid. Therefore, we use a reranker with a cross-encoder to
rank the retrieved documents according to the contextual-
ized query, using cross-attention over the query and docu-
ments. The reranker emits a relevance score for each docu-
ment. Documents that fall below the threshold are discarded

2. LITERATURE REVIEW
2.1
ENSO Prediction: From Dynamical Models to Deep Learning
The prediction of ENSO has been a central challenge in climate science for over four
decades. Early approaches relied on linear statistical models and dynamical ocean-
atmosphere coupled models [4]. While dynamical models based on General Circulation
Models (GCMs) can capture the fundamental physics of ENSO, their predictive skill
drops sharply beyond 6–9 months due to the well-documented “spring predictability
barrier,” where forecast initialization errors grow rapidly during boreal spring [3].
The advent of machine learning and deep learning has opened new avenues for ENSO
prediction. Unlike dynamical models that solve physical equations numerically, data-
driven approaches learn complex nonlinear mappings directly from observational data,
potentially capturing dynamics that are poorly represented in physical models.
2.2
Deep Learning Approaches for ENSO Forecasting

Step 2: Development of User Interface and Backend
A responsive frontend (React.js/TypeScript) is developed to allow user interaction,
while a FastAPI backend handles request processing, API routing, and communication between
modules. This layer acts as the central control system, ensuring smooth data flow between the UI
and AI components.
Step 3: Implementation of AI Council (Multi-Agent System)
A multi-agent framework is implemented where specialized agents (Tutor, RAG,
Evaluation, Memory, etc.) collaboratively process queries. The orchestrator coordinates these
agents to improve reasoning quality and generate structured responses instead of relying on a
single model.
Step 4: Hybrid RAG Pipeline Development
A Hybrid Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline is developed to enhance
response accuracy. This includes:
•
Vector database setup (FAISS/ChromaDB)
•
Context retrieval and reranking
•
Filtering and validation of retrieved data

3. PL/SQL
3.1 Triggers
3.1.1 Code
def create_audit_trigger():
connection = None
cursor = None
try:
connection = get_connection(db_name=DATABASE_NAME)
if connection is None:
return
cursor = connection.cursor()

print("\nCreating audit table if it doesn't exist...")
cursor.execute("""
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS audit_log (
Audit_ID INT AUTO_INCREMENT PRIMARY KEY,
Event_Type ENUM('INSERT', 'UPDATE', 'DELETE') NOT NULL,
Table_Name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,
Event_Time DATETIME NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
);
""")
print("Audit table created or already exists.")

cursor.execute("SHOW TABLES;")
tables = cursor.fetchall()

excluded_tables = ['audit_log'] # IMPORTANT: Do not trigger on the audit table itself

for table_dict in tables:
table_name = list(table_dict.values())[0] # Get the table name

if table_name in excluded_tables:
print(f"Skipping trigger creation for '{table_name}' (excluded).")
continue

general-purpose subagents, which work independently with
access to web and storage tools. After all subagents com-
plete their tasks, the parent or supervisor agent merges the
responses and serves the result to the user. Subagents per-
form tasks in parallel and asynchronously. The motivation
for this multi-agent design is to support complex queries
through dialectical reasoning. A single language-model re-
sponse, however fluent, is the result of one decoding pass
through one model’s priors; by contrast, the council forces
the system to consider the same question under several dis-
tinct epistemic constraints and then reconcile the resulting
drafts. This does not guarantee correctness; one perspective
is explicitly tasked with challenging the drafts produced by
the others. Although the user receives a multi-perspective
answer, the additional reasoning increases latency.
The council is implemented as a separate agent, but in
the main workflow it is represented as a single orchestrator

what follows.
For every (size, distribution) combination, a single input file containing exactly that many
integers is generated and written to disk. The benchmark then reads the file at run time and
feeds every algorithm an identical byte sequence. The use of on-disk files is the mechanism that
guarantees inter-algorithm fairness: there is no possibility that one algorithm is given a subtly
different input from another.
The random-number generator is seeded with a fixed value of 42 so that the random inputs
are reproducible across runs. Each random integer is constructed by concatenating two draws
from the standard library’s random-number generator; this widens the effective value range
beyond the library’s natural range. The random integers are then reduced modulo a multiple of
the input size so that the value range scales with the input rather than staying constant.
The three distributions are:

2.8
RAPTOR
RAPTOR introduces a way to retrieve information using a
tree structure built through recursive clustering and summa-
rization [8]. This method groups document data into layers,
each representing a level of detail. When a query comes in,
the system chooses the layer to search based on what the
query needs.
This layered approach keeps both details and broader con-
text in mind. It works well with long documents, where key
points might be spread out across different parts. Having
information organized like this helps find the relevant parts
faster and more accurately.
There is a downside. Building the tree takes a lot of com-
puting power: the process of clustering and summarizing

2. Modular RAG Frameworks:
Frameworks like
FlashRAG [3] split the system into modular stages—
such as rewriting questions, finding files, and sorting
results—making it simple to test any single part inde-
pendently.
3. Graph-Based and Hierarchical Representations: To
support multi-hop reasoning, GraphRAG [4] and Ligh-
tRAG [6] extract entity-relation graphs, HippoRAG [5]
applies Personalized PageRank over associative graphs,
and RAPTOR [8] builds recursive summary trees for
multi-scale retrieval.
4. Memory-Guided Context Management:
Standard
RAG pipelines treat each conversational turn indepen-
dently. In contrast, MemoRAG [7] clears up confusing
follow-ups by remembering earlier parts of the chat, us-
ing context clues to track down the right passages.

Problem Formulation and Research Gaps
While the systems summarized above advance retrieval ac-
curacy, they do not address the operational realities of run-
ning a personalized, multi-session tutor on local edge hard-

and an auxiliary Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) branch. Joint multi-task training covers all
nine SpeechOcean762 assessment labels using regression, correlation-based, binary classification, and CTC
objectives. On the test split, HiPPA achieves Pearson correlation coefficients (PCCs) of 0.396 for phoneme-
level assessment, 0.432 for word-total assessment, 0.754 for sentence fluency, 0.737 for sentence prosody, and
0.697 for sentence total. Sentence-fluency performance is comparable to GOPT (0.754 versus 0.753), while
sentence-prosody and sentence-total scores remain lower than those of several prior systems. HiPPA’s princi-
pal contribution is its unified end-to-end architecture, which combines reference-conditioned alignment with
explicit hierarchical information propagation across linguistic levels. The results demonstrate the feasibility
of jointly modelling pronunciation assessment at multiple granularities within a single framework.

However, performance on word-stress predic-
tion remains weak, with a Pearson correlation of
only 0.125. One likely explanation is label imbal-
ance within the dataset: most word-stress annotations
correspond to correct stress placement, resulting in a
highly skewed label distribution. Such imbalance re-
duces the ability of regression-based learning objec-
tives to differentiate subtle stress errors. The binary
classification formulation partially mitigates this; the
residual gap is consistent with prior reports on the
same dataset (Chen et al., 2024).

performance (0.396 PCC), it does not match GOPT’s
0.612 PCC (Gong et al., 2022).

fundamental challenge in pronunciation assessment
research. Fine-grained phoneme evaluation requires

Experiments
This section describes the implemented prototype, the work-
flows that were exercised during development, and the pre-
liminary functional observations obtained.
The labelled
evaluation battery that quantifies the retrieval (Static-k on
four BEIR golden datasets) and generation (Dynamic-k via
DeepEval on a curator-verified set of 38 items) layers is re-
ported separately in Section 5.
4.1
Prototype Implementation
The prototype consists of a FastAPI backend that hosts the
RagAgent and the AI Council subgraph, a sandboxed Elec-
tron desktop client for learner interaction, a Manifest V3
browser side-panel included in the repository as an unpacked
extension that talks to the backend directly, and a Docker
Compose configuration that coordinates PostgreSQL with
pgvector, Redis, FAISS-based per-session indexing, and
Ollama-served language and embedding models. The em-
bedding model used during the development was the gemma
family embedding model served through Ollama. Hugging

baseline — eight algorithms in total. A sample of 12 input files was selected by systematic

3.3
Data Collection
All seven textbook algorithms were implemented in C from the textbook descriptions, with no
use of any library sort routine. Each function takes an optional counter struct; when that struct is
non-null, the function records every comparison and every element move or swap it performs.
The counters are zero-cost when the struct is null, so the same code powers both a minimal demo

moves; it runs in Θ(𝑛2) worst case but in Θ(𝑛) on already-sorted input. Merge sort divides the
input in half, recursively sorts each half, then merges them with an auxiliary buffer of size Θ(𝑛);
it has worst-case Θ(𝑛log 𝑛). Quicksort selects a pivot, partitions, and recurses on each side; the
textbook first-element or last-element pivot variant has 𝑂(𝑛2) worst case, which is avoided in our
implementation by a uniformly random pivot. Heap sort builds the input into a max-heap and
repeatedly extracts the maximum; it has worst-case Θ(𝑛log 𝑛). Counting sort scans the input
once, allocates a count array indexed by value, and writes back the buckets in order; it runs in
𝑂(𝑛+ 𝑘) time, where 𝑘is the size of the value range, and uses 𝑂(𝑘) auxiliary memory. Together,
these descriptions agree on the asymptotic ranking that drives most pedagogical choices of
algorithm.

2. ChatGPT – OpenAI
ChatGPT is a conversational AI system developed by OpenAI that uses large
language models to generate human-like responses. The system can answer questions,
explain concepts, assist with programming tasks, and help with writing or research
activities. Because of its strong natural language capabilities, it is widely used by students
for learning support.
The conversational interface allows users to ask follow-up questions and receive
explanations in simple language. This makes it useful for quickly understanding complex
topics. However, ChatGPT typically provides responses based on general knowledge rather
than adapting to a specific user’s learning materials or progress.
Advantages
•
Strong conversational ability and clear explanations.
•
Useful for answering questions across many subjects.
Limitations
•
Limited personalization based on user learning materials.
•
Does not track student progress or provide structured learning evaluation tools.

2.9
Self-RAG
Self-RAG presents a way to combine retrieving information
and creating responses. The language model learns when
to retrieve information and how to check its own answers
[9]. This means the model can fetch information as needed
during response generation and then verify that the produced
response is correct.
One big benefit of Self-RAG is that it can change how it
works and think about its actions. It does not need to get
information all the time. It can check if the answers it gives
are right and fit the question.
The extra steps of making decisions and checking itself
make the process more complicated. Doing these steps can
also make the system take longer and use more computer
power.
SaviraNous uses a way to make sure things are reliable.
It sends questions to the place, gets information, checks it
again, improves it, and makes sure the final answer is cor-
rect. Even though this is not exactly the same as Self-RAG,

4.2.1
Path Judger Experiments
The Path Judger was exercised with three major categories
of queries. First, direct queries that did not require retrieval
were routed to the parametric- knowledge-only path and pro-
duced results without invoking the retriever.
Second, resource-grounded queries against an uploaded
corpus were routed to retrieval paths, and the responses
cited information drawn from the relevant resources rather
than from parametric knowledge. Lastly, context-dependent
queries that referenced prior turns were routed through the
working-context-aware paths and resolved their antecedents
correctly without requiring the user to repeat prior context.
4.2.2
AI Council Experiments

quests. When invoked, the council produced multi-section

• Niño 3.4 Index Computation
The target variable for the El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) forecasting objec-
tive is the Niño 3.4 index, defined as the area-averaged Sea Surface Temperature (SST)
anomaly over the Niño 3.4 region (5◦S–5◦N, 170◦W–120◦W). The index is computed di-
rectly from ERA5 SST anomaly fields, which are already ingested as part of the primary
dataset pipeline, thereby eliminating the need for an additional observational dataset.
For each monthly time step t, the Niño 3.4 index is computed as the area-weighted
spatial mean of SST anomalies over all grid cells (i, j) falling within the Niño 3.4
domain:

Despite significant progress in ENSO forecasting using deep learning, existing ap-
proaches face key limitations:
1. Most models predict only the Niño 3.4 index without translating predictions into
regional climate impacts, limiting their practical utility for disaster preparedness
and agricultural planning.
2. No unified deep learning framework currently exists that couples ENSO state
forecasting with deterministic regional impact assessment specifically for South
Asian precipitation and temperature anomalies.
This project addresses these gaps by developing a multi-task CNN-TCN framework that
forecasts ENSO state at 6 month lead times and simultaneously produces deterministic
precipitation and temperature anomaly maps over South Asia.

2.7
MemoRAG
MemoRAG brings a memory-enhanced retrieval method
where a memory module creates clues that direct the re-
trieval process [7]. The goal is to make retrieval better when
the user’s question is not clear, incomplete, or needs infor-
mation that is spread out over a long text.
The memory part can help the system understand the pic-
ture behind a question before getting specific details. This
can make the context match better and help with reasoning
through documents.
The success of this method depends a lot on how good
the memory clues are. The extra step of processing memory
also adds work for the computer.
This idea is important for SaviraNous because the sys-
tem keeps track of learner-related details and past conversa-

Retrieval Planner
Orchestration
Determines a per-resource retrieval budget instead of using

Orchestration / Retrieval

Literature Survey
Artificial Intelligence has become an important part of modern educational
technology. Many AI-based tools are now used to help students understand concepts,
summarize study materials, and answer questions. These systems aim to make learning
more interactive by allowing users to communicate with AI through natural language.
Several AI learning assistants and intelligent tutoring systems have been developed
to support students during their studies. However, many of these tools still have limitations
such as lack of personalization, limited tracking of learning progress, and concerns related
to data privacy. This section reviews three existing systems—NotebookLM, ChatGPT,
and AutoTutor—to understand their capabilities and identify the gaps that the proposed
system aims to address.

when necessary. The purpose of LTM is to support surface-

The literature backs the idea that query routing, retrieval,
memory and response generation should be treated as sepa-
rate yet cooperating parts.
3
System Architecture and Its Imple-
mentation
The proposed SaviraNous architecture is a layered, request-
oriented pipeline in which a query passes through a se-
quence of cooperating stages before reaching the generative
model. The query lifecycle begins when the user submits
a request and proceeds through query classification, hier-
archical resource routing, dynamic retrieval planning, se-
mantic retrieval, reranking, context construction, generation,
and memory updates. Each stage addresses a limitation of
monolithic retrieval-augmented generation, including con-

Source threads remain unchanged. When a checkpoint is

does not constitute a quantitative evaluation of retrieval qual-

3.3
Resource Processing and RAG Pipeline
The resource-processing pipeline grounds responses in
learner-supplied material. It is hierarchical: the system rea-
sons about resources before reasoning about their chunks,
so the cost of detailed retrieval is paid only after relevant
resources have been identified.
3.3.1
Resources Ingestion
When a learner introduces resources such as PDFs, YouTube
videos, directories, webpages, or images, they are first con-
verted into text and then into embeddings. For documents,
the content is extracted and split into chunks using resource-
aware chunkers: structure-aware chunkers for documents

Ltotal = α · LENSO + (1 −α) · Limpact
(3-14)
where LENSO is the Mean Squared Error (MSE) loss between the predicted and observed
Niño 3.4 index values, Limpact is the MSE loss between the predicted and observed South

on the validation set (2019–2022) to balance the contributions of both tasks. Gradi-
ents from both loss terms propagate back through the shared spatiotemporal encoder
simultaneously, enabling the encoder to learn representations that are jointly useful for
ENSO state forecasting and South Asian regional impact assessment.

5
Conclusion
The contribution of this paper is empirical. Seven textbook sorting algorithms plus one production
sort (C++ std::sort) were implemented, instrumented where instrumentation was possible,
and benchmarked on a single workstation against three input distributions across four input sizes
ranging from 102 to 105. Four findings emerge from the data.
First, the input distribution matters as much as the input size. Insertion sort is unusable on
reverse-sorted data (5 178 ms at 𝑁= 105) and the fastest algorithm on sorted data (0.20 ms
at the same 𝑁). Counting sort is fast on dense-value inputs and useless on wide-value inputs.
Big-O alone does not capture the situation.
Second, the three textbook 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) algorithms are not interchangeable. The fastest on
random data is consistently faster than the slowest by a factor of two, and the wall-clock difference
is traceable to the write count and the resulting cache behaviour.

3.4
Data Analysis
The collected data were analysed quantitatively. The wall-clock time of every algorithm on
every (size, distribution) cell is reported as a single number in milliseconds (Tables 1, 2, 3).
Comparison and write counts are reported as multiples of 𝑁and 𝑁log2 𝑁in the discussion
below. Auxiliary memory is reported in kilobytes (Table 4). The headline conclusions of the
paper are expressed as ratios of wall-clock times between pairs of algorithms on identical inputs
(e.g., std::sort versus merge sort, or insertion sort on sorted versus reverse-sorted input);
these ratios are robust to the run-to-run noise inherent in single-run timing. Tables and figures
were generated from the benchmark CSV output. No statistical aggregation (median of multiple

Content:
Previous empirical studies of sorting algorithms have primarily focused on measuring the
textbook sorts in isolation, or have characterised a single production algorithm in detail. Limited
research has examined the simultaneous comparison of all seven textbook sorts together with
a production standard-library sort on the same benchmark harness, with identical controlled
inputs, over multiple input distributions and multiple input sizes, and with the textbook sorts
instrumented to count both comparisons and writes. In particular, the empirical gap between
textbook merge sort and the production std::sort — a factor of ∼2.5 on random inputs and
up to ∼16 on sorted inputs at 𝑁= 105 — is not commonly presented in classroom material or
in surveys of standard-library sort choices. Therefore, this study aims to address this gap by
implementing, instrumenting, and benchmarking all seven textbook sorts and C++ std::sort

Ye et al. [2] developed ResoNet, a hybrid deep learning framework combining Residual
Network (ResNet) architecture with physical ocean dynamics for robust and explainable
ENSO forecasting. The model integrates residual learning blocks that capture multi-
scale spatiotemporal features from SST, ocean heat content, and wind stress fields. A
key contribution is the attention mechanism that identifies physically meaningful pre-
cursor regions.
ResoNet achieved state-of-the-art performance with correlation skills exceeding 0.7 at
12-month lead time and maintained useful skill (> 0.5) beyond 18 months. The ex-
plainability module generates spatial attribution maps highlighting the oceanic regions
most influential for each prediction, enhancing trust in the model’s outputs. The model
demonstrated particular strength in predicting the diversity of El Niño events (Eastern
Pacific vs. Central Pacific types), a capability lacking in many earlier deep learning ap-

3. Faculty and Course (1:N Relationship)
•
Tables Required: faculty, courses
•
A faculty member can teach only one course, but a course can be taught by multiple faculty
members.
•
The faculty table includes course_id as a foreign key.

4. Courses and Exams (1:N Relationship)
•
Tables Required: courses, exams
•
A course can conduct multiple exams, but each exam is associated with only one course.
•
The exam table includes course_id as a foreign key and exam_id as the primary key.
•
A unique constraint is applied to the combination of course_id and type.

5. Courses and Results (1:N Relationship)
•
Tables Required: courses, results
•
A course can have multiple results published, but each result belongs to only one course.
•
The results table includes course_id as a foreign key.

3.4.1
Data Ingestion
All datasets are aggregated, resampled to a consistent monthly 2°×2° grid, and aligned
to the common period 1980–2025. Data quality checks and gap-filling procedures are
applied to ensure temporal continuity.
3.4.2
Data Preprocessing
The raw climate datasets ingested from ERA5, ORAS5, and NOAA undergo the fol-
lowing preprocessing steps prior to feature engineering:
• Spatial Standardization
All datasets are ingested at their native 1◦latitude × 1◦longitude spatial resolution.
A 1◦latitude × 1◦longitude grid cell covers approximately 111 km (along latitude) ×
111 km (along longitude) at the equator, representing an area of roughly 12,300 km2.
At 27◦N (Nepal/India region), 1◦longitude shrinks to approximately 99 km, making
each 1◦latitude × 1◦longitude cell approximately 111 km × 99 km ≈10,989 km2. All
datasets are subsequently coarsened to a 2◦latitude × 2◦longitude grid using block

Content:
• Rust’s slice::sort_unstable uses ipnsort and stable slice::sort uses driftsort
[8]. The unstable-sort switch to ipnsort landed in Rust 1.81; the stable-sort switch to
driftsort landed in a later release (Rust 1.82/1.83).
• GNU C Library (qsort) attempts to use an auxiliary-memory merge-sort implementation
and falls back to a slower algorithm if the required memory cannot be allocated [10].
A pattern is visible across these designs.
When the situation demands stability, the
library picks a merge-sort-family algorithm (Timsort), with insertion sort built in for the
small-subproblem case. When the situation tolerates an unstable sort, the library picks a
quicksort-family algorithm and adds a heap-sort fallback so that the worst case cannot exceed
𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛). The two families have not been replaced — they have been stitched together. These
findings confirm the design intuition reported in the original Timsort, dual-pivot, and pdqsort
papers [4, 5, 7].

The results suggest that hierarchical representa-
tion learning provides advantages when modelling
pronunciation features that depend on global contex-
tual structure rather than isolated phonetic articula-
tion, but that these advantages alone are insufficient
to reach state-of-the-art sentence-level performance
without prosodic features or cross-granularity inter-
action.
5.2
Word-Level Prediction Behaviour
The model demonstrates moderate success in word-
level pronunciation scoring, achieving 0.432 Pearson
correlation for word-total prediction. This indicates
that aggregating phoneme-level acoustic representa-
tions into word-level embeddings successfully cap-
tures local pronunciation quality at intermediate lin-
guistic granularity.
However, performance on word-stress predic-
tion remains weak, with a Pearson correlation of

own recursion depth and switches to heap sort when the depth grows excessive, with insertion
sort used below a small fixed size; it is used by C++’s std::sort and is the production baseline
measured directly in this study. Pattern-Defeating Quicksort (pdqsort) [7] is a quicksort that

by Go since Go 1.19. Rust’s slice::sort_unstable uses ipnsort and stable slice::sort
uses driftsort [8]. The GNU C Library’s qsort uses a merge-sort variant with insertion sort
for small subproblems, with a fallback when memory cannot be allocated [10]. The detailed
mechanisms of these algorithms are discussed in Section 4.2.

Stage 1 : Spatial Encoder (CNN): A two-dimensional Convolutional Neural Network
(CNN) is applied independently to each monthly snapshot within the 12-month input
window. For each time step t, the CNN receives a spatial map of shape (Nϕ × Nλ × C)
where Nϕ = 30 denotes the number of latitude grid cells, Nλ = 100 denotes the number
of longitude grid cells, and C = 4 denotes the number of input channels (SST anomaly,
Sea Level Pressure (SLP) anomaly, wind stress anomaly, and upper 300m Ocean Heat
Content (OHC) anomaly). The CNN applies learned convolutional filters across the
spatial dimensions to extract physically meaningful spatial features.
The output of the spatial encoder for each time step t is a compact spatial feature vec-
tor f(t) ∈Rd, where d denotes the number of learned spatial features. Applying the
CNN independently and identically across all 12 time steps yields a sequence of spatial
feature vectors:
F = [f(1), f(2), . . . , f(12)] ∈R12×d

2.10
Comparative Analysis
The reviewed approaches tackle limitations found in tradi-
tional RAG systems. Adaptive-RAG works by choosing a
retrieval strategy based on how complex the query is. Cor-
rective RAG examines the retrieved information and im-
proves it by identifying and fixing errors. FlashRAG values
modularity in its design. GraphRAG and HippoRAG both
use graph structures to help the system reason about rela-
tionships and perform multi-hop reasoning.
LightRAG focuses on making retrieval faster and more ef-
ficient. MemoRAG uses memory to help guide the retrieval
process by providing hints. RAPTOR introduces a hierar-
chical way of representing documents. Self-RAG brings re-

LTM is implemented as a vector-addressable store of
small, semantically self-contained memory units. The store
supports insertion, similar reading and bounded k retrieval.
Since LTM is consulted on every turn, two design choices
are particularly important. First, the LTM payload presented
to the LLM is bounded by the filter that collects the relevant
memories against the current query and select the top N en-
tries. This creates the boundary for the memory section of
prompt regardless of how many memories user has accumu-
lated over the months of usage, this property allows the LTM
to remain useful at scale. Second, LTM writes are governed
by an explicit extraction stage rather than appending every
conversation turn. The memory-extraction agent identifies

(d) Tier 4 (LTM and Learner Profile): LTM (Long-
Term Memory) is the long-term store of sta-
ble learner-related facts (preferences, recurring
misconceptions, mastered concepts) that rarely
change, while the Learner Profile is a per-topic
skill model that is gradually updated via an Ex-
ponential Moving Average (α = 0.7) over the
course of the conversation.
Both are persis-
tent across sessions and user-scoped, but LTM
is write-rare and the Learner Profile is write-
frequent (with EMA smoothing).
Checkpointed session state supports restore, fork,
merge, and rewind operations while user-scoped LTM
remains outside thread-level rewinding.

7. FEASIBILITY ANALYSIS
7.1
Technical Feasibility
The project is technically feasible given the availability of all required datasets through
public repositories (ECMWF CDS, NOAA, ESGF). The Convolutional Neural Network
– Temporal Convolutional Network (CNN-TCN) architecture is well-supported in Py-
Torch, and the 45-year dataset (1980–2025) provides sufficient temporal coverage for
robust model training. The six-stage pipeline (data ingestion, preprocessing, feature
engineering, MIFS feature selection, model development, and forecasting) is modular
and implementable within the team’s existing Python and machine learning skill set.
7.2
Economic Feasibility
The project incurs near-zero data and software costs, as all datasets and development
tools are freely available. The primary expenditure is cloud compute (Google Colab
Pro) for CNN-TCN training on ERA5 and ORAS5 reanalysis fields. The total estimated
budget of NPR 10,500 is well within the means of a student project group, with no

3.2
Acoustic Feature Encoding
The raw audio sequence is passed through a pre-
trained WavLM encoder (Chen et al., 2022) to obtain
contextual speech representations. Given input audio
X, the encoder generates a stack of hidden acoustic
states Hi ∈RT×d for i = 1,...,L. Rather than relying
solely on the final layer, the model learns a softmax-
weighted combination of intermediate hidden states
to preserve both low-level phonetic cues and higher-
level contextual speech information, as in (Chen et al.,
2022):
Hfinal =
L
∑
i=1
αiHi,
αi =
exp(wi)
∑L
j=1 exp(w j)
(2)
where L is the number of encoder layers, d is the hid-
den dimension, and wi are learnable scalar weights.

CLOCK_MONOTONIC on POSIX builds. The benchmark harness initialises the timer once at
start-up, takes a timestamp just before the algorithm call, takes another just after, and reports the
difference in milliseconds.

Experimental evaluation on the SpeechOcean762
benchmark shows the framework reaching 0.754
Pearson correlation for sentence fluency, 0.737 for
prosody, 0.697 for sentence-level total scoring, 0.432
PCC for word-total scoring, and 0.396 PCC for
phone accuracy.
Relative to prior systems on the
same benchmark, HiPPA is essentially at parity with
GOPT on sentence fluency (0.754 vs. 0.753) but trails
GOPT on sentence prosody (0.737 vs. 0.760) and
sentence total (0.697 vs. 0.742); it also trails the
more recent MuFFIN, HierGAT, and MTP systems
on every sentence-level metric reported in Table 1.

trails GOPT (0.612) and MuFFIN (0.742) because
of HiPPA’s reliance on learned cross-attention rather

• Fastest versus slowest within the 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) class at 𝑁= 105 random inputs. std::sort
at 9.92 milliseconds and merge sort at 24.99 milliseconds give a ratio of 2.5. Within the
same asymptotic class, the production sort is two-and-a-half times faster than the textbook
merge sort on random inputs at 𝑁= 105.
• Insertion sort on sorted versus reverse-sorted input, at 𝑁= 105. Sorted input 0.20
milliseconds; reverse-sorted input 5 178 milliseconds. The ratio is approximately 2.6×104.
Same algorithm, same size, more than four orders of magnitude of difference, driven
entirely by the distribution of the input.
• std::sort versus merge sort across all three input distributions. At 𝑁= 105, the
production sort is faster than the textbook merge sort by factors of 2.5 (random), 16.4
(sorted), and 15.7 (reverse-sorted). The production sort is strictly faster on every cell.

2.1.2 ER To Table
The University Management System requires a well-structured database with 12 (including two
tables for multivalued attributes of students and faculty) tables to handle various entities and their
relationships. The relationships between entities are outlined below:
1. Students and Courses (N:M Relationship)
•
Tables Required: students, courses, enrollment
•
Students can enroll in multiple courses, and a course can have multiple students.
•
The enrollment table establishes the relationship, with a composite primary key comprising
student_id and course_id.
•
An additional attribute, enrollment_date, is included.

2. Faculty and Department (1:N Relationship)
•
Tables Required: faculty, department
•
A faculty member belongs to a single department, but a department can have multiple faculty
members.
•
The department table is independent, while the faculty table includes department_id as a foreign
key.

4. Departments and Courses:
•
Total Participation (Both): Every department must offer at least one course, and every course
must be offered by a department. Thus, both department and course participation are total.

•

•

participate totally, while students participate partially.

exam have associated fees, but some fees may not be related to any specific course or exam

5.1
Motivation: From Fixed-k to Dynamic-k
The motivation for the planner-driven dynamic-k design
comes from observing the precision–recall trade-off that
emerges when retrieval depth is held fixed. In the earliest
SaviraNous configuration, every retrieve only query
was sent through retrieval with a single global budget k. The
behaviour of that fixed-k engine on the four BEIR datasets
in Section 5.2 makes the problem visible on its own.
When k was set too small, the retriever surfaced only
the top few chunks per query. The selected chunks were
tight — often correct on the literal facts — so the engine
could reliably answer direct factual questions, and Contex-
tual Precision stayed high. But the small budget starved re-
call: any question whose gold evidence ranked below the
cut-off, multi-hop questions that need a second supporting
passage, and broader topical queries were unanswerable.

The council is implemented as a separate agent, but in
the main workflow it is represented as a single orchestrator
node. It can access the active Hippocampus, PFC and LTM
from the parent state and write the response back to turn
messages in active chat window. Since the council runs as
a subgraph, the rest of the system does not need to know
whether a given turn was answered by a single model call
or by a council. From the perspective of downstream nodes,
the AI Council is a black box that emits a response. Its task-
decomposition, parallel-subagent, and supervisory-merging
structure is shown in Figure 4.

Evaluation

SaviraNous RAG stack. They are deliberately separated be-
cause they exercise the system at different layers and with
different fidelities to the production engine.
The Static-k evaluation (Section 5.2) runs against
four public BEIR golden datasets (scifact, fiqa, nfcorpus,
musique), 100 queries each, with the top-k dense candidates
passed through the cross-encoder reranker per query. The
harness under SaviraNous-Evaluation/ reimplements only
the retrieval path (dense + optional cross-encoder rerank);
the planner and the agent decision loop are not exercised —
there is no planner in this path, and the retriever returns a
fixed k chunks from the vector store on each query rather
than receiving a planner-driven dynamic kalloc. Because the

3
METHODOLOGY
We
present
HiPPA
(Hierarchical
Multi-
Granularity Pronunciation Assessment), a unified
transformer-based framework designed to jointly pre-
dict pronunciation quality across phoneme, word, and
sentence levels. The architecture combines pretrained
acoustic
representations,
reference-conditioned
hierarchical encoding, cross-attention alignment, and
multi-task learning objectives to model pronunciation
quality across multiple linguistic granularities.
The overall architecture is designed to learn hi-
erarchical dependencies between low-level phonetic
articulation, word-level pronunciation quality, and
high-level sentence fluency and prosodic structure.

are
conducted

Background of the Study
Sorting algorithms have been a foundational subject of computer science since the earliest
textbooks [1, 2, 3]. They affect software performance in essentially every domain that processes
data: databases, operating systems, compilers, scientific computing, graphics, and machine-
learning pipelines all spend measurable fractions of their wall-clock time in sort routines.
Previous research has produced well-understood asymptotic classifications (𝑂(𝑛2) for the simple
sorts, 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) for the divide-and-conquer and heap-based sorts, and 𝑂(𝑛+ 𝑘) for counting
and radix sorts), and these classifications drive most pedagogical and many practical choices of

3.4.2
Hippocampus
As the working context grows, retaining every prior mes-
sage would eventually exceed the model’s context length and
contribute to context pollution. The Hippocampus addresses
this problem by acting as a compressed representation of
past messages. When the active chat window grows larger,
older turn messages (Human messages + AI/Tool messages)
are passed to the summarizer agent, and the resulting com-
pressed narrative is retained as a single rolling summary
which can be carried alongside the active chat window. The
Hippocampus is therefore not a verbatim log of what was
said. It is a compact, regeneratable description of what mat-
ters about what was said. The size of Hippocampus is also
bound. The summarization agent is invoked whenever the
Hippocampus approaches its context limit.
3.4.3
Prefrontal Cortex (PFC)
The PFC is structurally different from the Hippocampus. It
does not describe what the learner has said; rather, it de-

findings confirm the design intuition reported in the original Timsort, dual-pivot, and pdqsort
papers [4, 5, 7].
The empirical contribution of this paper is the measurement of one particular representative
of the second family, C++ std::sort, on the same harness as the textbook algorithms. The
measurements confirm two claims that the textbook leaves unsubstantiated. First, the gap between
the textbook merge sort and the production introsort is large — a factor of 2.5 on random inputs
at 𝑁= 105 and a factor of up to 16 on sorted inputs. Second, the introsort hybrid retains the
random-input speed of plain quicksort while patching the worst case: at 𝑁= 105 on random
inputs, the hand-rolled random-pivot quicksort and the production introsort are within seven
percent of each other on a single run.

Synthesis
The deeper answer is that the major libraries do not treat the selection of a sort algorithm as

Content:
This study is a quantitative experimental study (“testing relationships under controlled condi-
tions”). The controlled conditions are: a fixed set of eight sorting algorithms, a fixed set of input
sizes, a fixed set of input distributions, a fixed workstation, a fixed compiler toolchain, and a
fixed measurement protocol. Every algorithm is run on every (size, distribution) cell, and the
dependent variable (wall-clock time, plus the secondary variables of comparison count, write
count, and auxiliary memory) is recorded once per cell. The inclusion of C++ std::sort as a
production baseline alongside the textbook sorts makes this an experimental comparison rather
than a purely descriptive survey.
3.2
Population and Sample
The population of this study consists of the seven textbook sorting algorithms (bubble, selection,
insertion, merge, quick, heap, counting) together with C++ std::sort as the production

FUTURE SCOPE OF THE PROJECT
In real life, the projectile motion finds applications in sports. Playing basketball, football is
examples of projectile motion in real life. While throwing a basketball into the basket, the player
shoots the ball in such a way that the flight taken by the ball is in the form of a parabola.

when a basketball player aims to score a basket, he/she tends to hold a ball at an angle, and
he/she throws it. This causes the ball to move along the horizontal direction while rising in
height at the same time. Hence, due to the presence of both horizontal and vertical motion, a
projectile motion is said to be existing.

ˆp = fjudge(qt, St, Mt, It),
(1)
where qt is the current query, St is session-level signal (in-

this”), Mt is the available memory context, and It is the

emits a structured prediction (path label plus optional URL
list) that downstream nodes treat as a binding contract.
The judger emits a structured output (path + optional list
of resources that it needs to ingest) that downstream nodes
treat them as a binding contract.
This separation has two architectural consequences. First,
the retrieval planner, reranker, and generation modules are
not invoked unconditionally; they are engaged conditionally.
When a query does not require retrieval or ingestion, those
stages are skipped. Second, because the path is determin-
istic for the contextualized query, the path label becomes a
contract between stages. This allows downstream nodes to

Conclusion and Future Work
This paper has described SaviraNous, an integrated proto-
type that combines three contributions behind a per-turn
routing decision. SaviraNous uses a memory architecture
that includes the active chat window, the Hippocampus, the
Prefrontal Cortex, and persistent Long-Term Memory. Savi-
raNous assigns each tier lifetime semantics that are designed
to behave coherently under checkpoint restore, fork, and
thread merge, with LTM user-scoped and excluded from
thread-level rewinding. SaviraNous also includes a RAG
pipeline. In this pipeline, the Prefrontal Cortex performs
resource routing and the FAISS index performs chunk-level

METHODOLOGY
For studying the variation of range of cannon ball with the angle of projection the mentioned the
methods were followed which are as follows:
• Initial velocity was by default 15m/s which was changed to 12m/s
• The initial angle of projection was 800 and 250 was the minimum angle of projection
which we could set in the software.
• The angle of projection was changed gradually but the initial velocity was always
maintained constant.
• Then the range was observed as per the change in the angle of projection
Hence, the variation of range was with change with change in the angle was seen through the
technique.

Content:
This highlights a
fundamental challenge in pronunciation assessment
research. Fine-grained phoneme evaluation requires
highly accurate temporal alignment between learner
speech and expected phoneme boundaries.
GOPT
and the Kaldi GOP+SVR baseline both rely on forced
alignment using acoustic models trained explicitly for
phonetic discrimination; in contrast, HiPPA learns
contextual acoustic representations in an end-to-end
manner and approximates phoneme segments through
cross-attention rather than hard alignment.
A related limitation of SpeechOcean762 is the
absence of exact phoneme- and word-level times-
tamp annotations.
Since explicit segment bound-
aries are unavailable, HiPPA relies on approximate
proportional segmentation together with the cross-
attention alignment module and CTC auxiliary super-
vision. This introduces uncertainty during phoneme-
level representation learning that GOP-feature-based
systems such as GOPT do not face.

trieval over conversational, paragraph-length context.
nfcorpus (BEIR) is a biomedical information-retrieval
benchmark built on PubMed titles/abstracts [21]. Queries
are short (avg. 3–4 tokens) and the corpus is biased to-
ward medical terminology. Retrieval here exercises a tight
precision-at-1 regime (many queries have a single relevant
document), which is why nDCG@1 is unusually high but
recall@10 stays low.
musique (Multi-hop QA) is multi-hop question answer-
ing over Wikipedia paragraphs [22]. Each question requires
chaining two or more supporting passages to reach the gold
answer. Unlike the BEIR single-hop tasks above, musique
specifically probes whether the retriever can surface all sup-
porting passages at depth rather than just the top hit, and it
stresses cross-passage reasoning.

SaviraNous Local-First AI Tutor With Multi-Agent Reasoning
Sajid Miya
Department of Computer Science and Engineering
Thapar Institute of Engineering and Technology, Patiala, Punjab, India
smiya60 be23@thapar.edu
Abstract
Conversational AI assistants built directly on top of a single
large language model offer fluency but lack three properties
that a learning-oriented tutor requires: structured long-term
context about the learner, hierarchical reasoning over het-
erogeneous learning resources, and grounding of generated
answers in those resources. We describe SaviraNous, an in-
tegrated prototype that addresses these limitations through
three architectural contributions. First, a hierarchical mem-
ory architecture separates the active working context, a com-
pressed Hippocampus narrative, a resource-level Prefrontal
Cortex (PFC), and a persistent Long-Term Memory (LTM);
the four layers have distinct lifetimes and read/write se-
mantics, with session-layer state managed through check-

1.4.3. Limitations (Out of Scope)
•
The system does not include modules for Attendance Tracking, Hostel Management, or Library
Management.
•
Integration with external learning platforms (e.g., Moodle, Blackboard) is not covered.
•
Advanced reporting, analytics, and data visualization features are not implemented.
•
Real-time payment gateway integration is excluded; payments are simulated.
•
SMS notifications are not implemented.

recharge-discharge oscillator mechanism and delayed oscillator theory, as structural pri-

2.2
Deep Learning Approaches for ENSO Forecasting
2.2.1
CNN-Based Multi-Year ENSO Forecasting
Ham et al. [1] demonstrated a breakthrough in ENSO prediction by applying transfer
learning with CNNs to forecast the Niño 3.4 index at lead times exceeding 18 months.
Their approach addressed the fundamental challenge of limited observational data by
pre-training the CNN on historical simulations from CMIP5 climate models and fine-
tuning on reanalysis data (1871–1973 for training, 1984–2017 for testing). The CNN
architecture processes monthly maps of SST, ocean heat content (upper 300m), and sea
level pressure as multi-channel inputs, producing Niño 3.4 index predictions at various
lead times.
The model achieved an all-season correlation skill of 0.65 at 17-month lead time, sub-
stantially outperforming both dynamical models and conventional statistical methods.

4.2.5
Synthesis
The deeper answer is that the major libraries do not treat the selection of a sort algorithm as
a one-time decision. They treat it as a multi-criterion optimisation under the constraints of
stability, auxiliary-memory budget, worst-case behaviour, and the distribution properties of the
input. The seven textbook algorithms surveyed in this paper are best understood as the building
blocks of the production algorithms, not as alternatives to them. An algorithm is “fast” in the
textbook sense only when the input is well-behaved and the Big-O constants are favourable; in
production, “fast” is whatever algorithm wins on the input it is actually given, with the resources
it is actually given.
The data in this paper supports the rule of thumb that the major language communities appear
to have converged on:
1. For small 𝑁— the threshold is typically somewhere between four and thirty-two elements
— use insertion sort.

2
Literature Review
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a method that
helps large language models (LLMs) produce answers
grounded in data from external sources. Rather than relying
solely on the model’s parametric knowledge, a RAG system
retrieves relevant facts and provides them to the language
model as additional context. This grounding is particularly
useful when answers must remain tied to specific papers,
files, or subject-specific material.
A normal search pipeline is not always the best way
to answer every question. Easy questions might not need
a search at all while hard questions might need several
searches and thinking steps. New studies have looked at re-
trieval, retrieval correction, modular pipelines, graph-based
retrieval, memory-guided retrieval, hierarchical retrieval and
self-reflective generation. These ideas offer ways to make
searches better, help the model think, work faster and under-
stand context better.

A Comparative Empirical Study of Seven Classical
Sorting Algorithms
Plus C++ std::sort (Introsort): Why Standard Libraries Don’t Ship Plain
Merge Sort or Quicksort
Sajida Khatun∗, Susatya Khadka, Bishesta KC
Uniglobe College, Kathmandu, Nepal
{sajidakhatun82, susatyamkhadka82, bishestakc82}@uniglobecollege.edu.np
September 28, 2026
Abstract
This study investigates the empirical performance of fundamental sorting algorithms
with the objective of understanding how asymptotic complexity, input distribution, and
implementation characteristics influence practical sorting performance. The study focuses
on seven textbook sorting algorithms: bubble sort, selection sort, insertion sort, merge
sort, quicksort, heap sort, and counting sort, together with C++ std::sort as a production
baseline. All seven textbook algorithms were implemented from scratch in C, instrumented
to count comparisons and writes, and benchmarked on a single workstation using controlled

3.1
Dataset and Input Representation
Experiments
are

using
the
Spee-

5,000 utterances collected from 250 Mandarin-
speaking English learners, with expert annotations
at three linguistic levels. The official split provides
2,500 training utterances and 2,500 test utterances,
each with five expert annotators.
For each training sample, the input consists of raw
speech waveform, canonical text transcript, canoni-
cal phoneme sequence, word-level pronunciation la-
bels, sentence-level pronunciation labels, and speaker
metadata (age and gender).
The training pipeline first converts raw speech au-

X = {x1,x2,...,xT}

where X represents the temporal audio sequence of T
acoustic frames.

Operational Feasibility
The project delivers a self-contained Python-based forecasting pipeline publishable as

South Asian precipitation and temperature anomaly maps for JJAS 2026) are directly
interpretable and actionable for the target academic audience.

2.5
HippoRAG
HippoRAG proposes a neurobiologically inspired long-term
memory architecture for language models [5].
It uses a
knowledge graph with Personalized PageRank (PPR) to re-
trieve information that matches a query.
The graph-based memory representation lets information
link together and be pulled out based on the relationships
inside the knowledge structure. This is why HippoRAG is
especially useful for long-term and multi-hop information
retrieval.

AI Council Experiments
The council path was invoked only through explicit user re-
quests. When invoked, the council produced multi-section

drafts, with an aggregated final answer produced by the
merger. CPU and wall-clock costs increased substantially
compared with the single-model path, as expected.

Content:
The structural reason for these results is the design of the algorithms themselves (Section 3):
the three quadratic algorithms have a content-agnostic inner loop (selection sort) or a content-
aware inner loop whose input-size scaling is dominated by the worst case (bubble sort, insertion
sort). The four 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) algorithms all have worst-case 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) performance, but their
constant factors differ by a factor of two in some directions. Counting sort is the only algorithm
whose cost depends on the value range rather than the input size, and its performance therefore
depends on the input distribution in a way that none of the other seven algorithms do. These
findings support the existing literature [1, 2] on the asymptotic ranking, but show that the
asymptotic ranking alone under-specifies the wall-clock ordering on real data.
4.2.2
Why standard libraries don’t ship plain merge sort
The textbook merge sort has three properties that limit its attractiveness for production use.

to have converged on:
1. For small 𝑁— the threshold is typically somewhere between four and thirty-two elements
— use insertion sort.
2. For large 𝑁on random data, use a quicksort variant with a fallback, namely introsort or
pdqsort.
3. For large 𝑁on real-world (often partially sorted) data, use a stable hybrid such as Timsort.
4. For very large 𝑁on small-valued integers, consider counting sort or radix sort [2].
The thresholds and the choice between stable and unstable are not mysterious; they fall out
of the measurements above and from the auxiliary-memory budgets of the typical call site.

Content:
every additional chunk consumes a non-trivial share of the
model’s context window, inflating per-turn prompt size, in-
ference latency, and dollar cost; and downstream generation
degradation — even correctly-retrieved extra chunks propa-
gate their noise into the synthesised answer.
A fixed retrieval budget therefore cannot satisfy both
regimes at once. The SaviraNous engine resolves this by
giving the planner control over k on a per-resource ba-
sis. Instead of a single global budget, the planner emits a
bounded per-resource retrieval plan kalloc whose magnitude
scales with the size of the candidate resource and the in-
ferred scope of the query. Small resources with a handful
of relevant chunks are not over-retrieved; large resources
with a broad question are allocated a larger budget. The
cross-encoder reranker and the configurable relevance floor
then prune low-confidence candidates before they reach the
prompt, so the precision side of the trade-off is preserved,

2.12
Positioning of SaviraNous
SaviraNous is positioned as a learning and memory sys-
tem that brings together ideas seen in the approaches we
reviewed.
SaviraNous does not try to copy any existing RAG archi-
tecture. Instead, SaviraNous blends chosen concepts into a
learning-oriented workflow.
The Path Judger provides query-dependent routing, a fea-
ture that is related to the retrieval principle discussed in
Adaptive-RAG.
The retrieval pipeline uses resource selection, semantic
retrieval, reranking, and refinement, showing the modular
processing ideas highlighted by FlashRAG.
SaviraNous also incorporates different kinds of context
and memory. The Active Chat Window keeps the recent
messages of the active conversation, the Hippocampus sup-
plies compressed historical conversational context, the Pre-

The textbook ranking of sorts by asymptotic class is agreed on by all the major references
[1, 2, 3] and matches the design choices made by the production libraries. The textbook and the
library-design literature disagree, however, on the weight given to three axes that are routinely
omitted from the Big-O table: auxiliary memory, cache friendliness, and sensitivity to input
shape. A sort that runs in 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) time but allocates an extra 𝑂(𝑛) bytes of memory is
unusable on constrained devices, so merge sort’s auxiliary buffer is a meaningful constraint in
4

Content:
2. Merge sort allocates two auxiliary buffers per recursive merge call. No attempt is made to
reuse a single shared buffer at the top level, so the memory figure reported for merge sort
is the worst-case value of two halves of the working segment, which simplifies to a total of
one array’s worth of auxiliary memory at any moment.
3. Counting sort refuses to allocate a count array whose size exceeds fifty million entries.
This safety net triggers on wide-value inputs. In the random-input runs at the largest size
it engages, and the algorithm returns without sorting. The consequence for the benchmark
output is discussed in Section 5.1 and again in Section 4.2; the relevant rows in the result
table are flagged with a dagger.
4. C++ std::sort is not instrumented. It is a black box from the harness’s perspective. Its
wall-clock time is measured faithfully but its comparison and write counts are not. This is
the reason std_sort does not appear in the comparisons and writes plots.

dependently projected to the response prompt, and the LLM
receives them as labelled sections. After the turn, active chat
window gets updated with the new exchange, the Hippocam-

active context. When the conversation grows, only the Hip-

window stays bounded. When the resources ingestion be-
comes large then only PFC grows while chunk index grows
in vectors. LTM growth is decoupled from both because
LTM is consulted with similarity rather than inclusion.

time but also at the level of state manipulation. The engine
maintains the checkpoint for each conversational thread al-
lowing operations like restore, fork, merge threads or rewind

3.4.5
Baseline Model
An eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) regression model is employed as the base-
line to establish a performance floor against which the proposed CNN-TCN is evaluated.
XGBoost operates on tabular input and therefore receives the MIFS-selected features
as a flattened one-dimensional feature vector per sample, discarding the spatial and
temporal structure preserved in the CNN-TCN input tensor.

The takeaway from the sorted column at 𝑁= 105 is that std::sort is 16.4 times faster
than merge sort on this input, 9.0 times faster than heap sort, and 5.3 times faster than quicksort.

sorted-input fast path that introsort’s hybrid does not match for this size.
4.1.3
Reverse-sorted inputs
Table 3 reports the timings on reverse-sorted inputs. This is the canonical worst case for insertion
sort, because every new element has to slide all the way to the front of the sorted prefix. Insertion
sort slows down to essentially its random-input running time. Counting sort is fast because the
value range of the reversed input is dense (the values are 𝑁−1, 𝑁−2, . . . , 0, all within the safety
threshold). std::sort is the second-fastest algorithm in this column, beating the hand-rolled
quicksort by a factor of 7.4.

2.6
LightRAG
LightRAG aims to make retrieval-augmented generation
more efficient [6]. It does this by mixing graph-based in-
formation with key-value memory and by using dual-level
retrieval, fetching keyword-level data while cutting down on
the amount of context that must be read.
The main benefit of LightRAG is efficiency: it is built
to reduce token usage and lower retrieval overhead, and it
allows updates, which makes it attractive for systems that
need speed and quick responses.
LightRAG has a drawback: its retrieval quality relies on
effective keyword extraction, and although it is efficient, it
may not offer the same depth of reasoning as heavier graph-
based methods.
SaviraNous also focuses on resource-aware retrieval.
SaviraNous does not scan the knowledge base for every
query.
SaviraNous first spots resources and then does a
deeper retrieval. This lets SaviraNous focus retrieval effort
