Content:
2
Literature Review
2.1
Textbook descriptions of the classical algorithms
The seven textbook sorting algorithms examined in this study are described in detail in the
standard algorithms textbooks of [1], [2], and [3]. Bubble sort repeatedly walks the input,
swapping adjacent pairs that are out of order and terminating early if a pass made no swap; it
runs in 𝑂(𝑛2). Selection sort repeatedly scans the unsorted suffix for the minimum element and
swaps it into the next position of the sorted prefix; it has no content-aware fast path and performs
the full 𝑂(𝑛2) work on every input distribution. Insertion sort walks the input left to right,
sliding each new element into its place among the already-sorted prefix by repeated adjacent
moves; it runs in Θ(𝑛2) worst case but in Θ(𝑛) on already-sorted input. Merge sort divides the
input in half, recursively sorts each half, then merges them with an auxiliary buffer of size Θ(𝑛);

5. CONCLUSION
The University Management System project successfully delivers a comprehensive web
application tailored for managing essential academic and administrative functions at Thapar
Institute. By utilizing the Flask framework, a MySQL database, and standard web technologies,
the system provides a centralized, efficient, and role-based platform for students, faculty, and
administrators. It effectively automates critical processes such as registration, enrollment, exam
management, fee tracking, and result processing, thereby reducing manual workload and the
potential for errors. The inclusion of features like automated email notifications, dynamic
credential generation, and percentile-based grading enhances its utility. The project
demonstrates a practical application of database design principles and full-stack web
development to address real-world challenges in university administration, providing a solid
foundation for future expansion and improvement.

wall-clock numbers are first-class measurements, but the comparison and write counts in the
plots and tables are not directly comparable with the textbook algorithms. Inferences about why
std::sort is faster (cache behaviour versus asymptotically fewer operations) cannot be drawn
from this data alone. Future research should instrument std::sort via libstdc++ debug
hooks, or use a custom introsort implementation with the same instrumentation, so that the
comparison and write counts become directly comparable.
Single-run timings.
Each cell in the tables above is the result of one run, not the median of
several. Wall-clock time has run-to-run jitter at the few-percent level, so the differences that the
paper highlights (for example, std::sort faster than merge sort on random data) are expected
to survive multiple runs but should not be over-interpreted in the second decimal place. Future
research should run 𝑁≥5 repetitions per cell and report medians with confidence intervals.

1. To develop a CNN-TCN model to forecast the Niño 3.4 index at a 6-month lead

1.5
Scope and Limitations
1.5.1
Scope
• The system will forecast ENSO states using the Niño 3.4 index at lead times of 6
months.
• Regional impact assessment will cover the South Asian domain (5◦N–40◦N, 60◦E–
100◦E).
• The model will be trained on publicly available datasets including ERA5 and
ORAS5.
• The final deliverable will include a functional forecasting pipeline implemented
in Python.
1.5.2
Limitations
• The model is trained on historical data and may not capture unprecedented cli-
mate dynamics.
• The system does not account for other climate drivers (e.g., Indian Ocean Dipole,
Arctic Oscillation) that may modulate ENSO impacts.

4.2 Create database
4.2.1 Code
def create_database():
connection = None
cursor = None
try:
connection = get_connection()
if connection is None:
return

cursor = connection.cursor()
print(f"\nCreating database `{DATABASE_NAME}` if it doesn't exist...")
cursor.execute(f"CREATE DATABASE IF NOT EXISTS `{DATABASE_NAME}`;")
print(f"Database `{DATABASE_NAME}` created or already exists.")

if cursor: cursor.close()
if connection: connection.close()

# Now connect to the specific database to create tables within it
connection = get_connection(db_name=DATABASE_NAME)
if connection is None:
# Connection to specific DB failed, exit setup
print("Could not connect to the newly created database. Exiting.", file=sys.stderr)
sys.exit(1)

cursor = connection.cursor()

Content:
Hippocampus approaches its context limit.
3.4.3
Prefrontal Cortex (PFC)
The PFC is structurally different from the Hippocampus. It
does not describe what the learner has said; rather, it de-
scribes the resources available to the learner during the ac-
tive chat. For every resource ingested by the learner, PFC
maintains a compact description for each resource.
The
PFC therefore behaves as a “map of the territory” over the
learner’s corpus, allowing the engine to reason about which
resources it needs to search before paying the cost of any
detailed retrieval. The PFC is preserved across the turns and
updated whenever the resources are ingested or purged.
3.4.4
Long-Term Memory (LTM)
LTM is the persistent cross-session layer. Unlike the Active
Chat Window, Hippocampus, and PFC, LTM is designed
to outlive any single interaction. The SaviraNous engine
stores persistent learner-related information in PostgreSQL
with pgvector extracted from the prior conversations and

2.5
Research Gap and Motivation
Despite the progress above, three issues remain open.
First, several systems model multi-granularity predic-
tion in parallel rather than as an explicit hierarchy,
which limits the model’s ability to propagate infor-
mation from finer to coarser linguistic units.
Sec-
ond, many systems rely on forced alignment between
learner speech and the canonical phonetic transcript,
which is unavailable in the SpeechOcean762 release.
Third, end-to-end integration of phoneme-, word-,
and sentence-level scoring with hierarchical aggrega-
tion is comparatively underexplored. These observa-
tions motivate the design choices behind HiPPA.
The proposed HiPPA framework addresses these
limitations by introducing an explicit hierarchi-
cal aggregation from phoneme to word to sen-
tence within a single transformer-based architecture,
with a reference-conditioned cross-attention align-
ment module providing a learned spoken–reference
alignment signal.
3

2. Asymmetric Four-Tier Cognitive Memory (Resolv-
ing Gap 2): To stop the computer from running out of
memory and losing track of the chat, SaviraNous re-
places flat message lists with four organized tiers:
(a) Tier 1 (Active Chat Window): Keeps the most
recent messages fresh and uncompressed for
smooth back-and-forth conversation.
(b) Tier 2 (Hippocampus): Keeps a short, updated
summary of past messages so the AI does not for-
get important details or waste memory.
(c) Tier 3 (Prefrontal Cortex / PFC): Organizes and
tracks all uploaded study notes and workspace
files.
(d) Tier 4 (LTM and Learner Profile): LTM (Long-
Term Memory) is the long-term store of sta-
ble learner-related facts (preferences, recurring

This iterative procedure ensures that each newly selected feature contributes maximum
new predictive information about the Niño 3.4 target beyond what the already selected
features collectively provide.
Physical Interpretation: The spatial regions expected to achieve the highest mutual in-
formation scores with the Niño 3.4 target are consistent with established ENSO physics.
Sea Surface Temperature (SST) anomalies in the central and eastern equatorial Pa-
cific, equatorial wind stress anomalies along the Walker Circulation pathway, and upper
300m Ocean Heat Content (OHC) anomalies in the western Pacific thermocline are ex-
pected to carry the strongest predictive signal. The redundancy penalization step further
ensures that highly correlated neighbouring grid cells, which carry largely overlapping
information, do not dominate the selected feature set, yielding a spatially diverse and
physically meaningful set of predictors.

4.2.2
Why standard libraries don’t ship plain merge sort
The textbook merge sort has three properties that limit its attractiveness for production use.
1. It uses an 𝑂(𝑛) auxiliary buffer. At 𝑁= 105 this is approximately four hundred kilobytes
in our measurements. For larger 𝑁on memory-constrained devices this is a meaningful
fraction of the available memory and forces a copy back and forth between the input and
the auxiliary buffer at every level of the recursion.
2. It performs the same number of writes regardless of the input distribution. This costs
roughly five times as many writes as quicksort on the same input (≈2𝑁log2 𝑁for merge
sort versus ≈0.4𝑁log2 𝑁for the random-pivot quicksort), and the wall-clock difference
between merge sort and std::sort in our measurements is large: on random inputs at
𝑁= 105, merge sort takes 24.99 milliseconds and std::sort takes 9.92 milliseconds;
on sorted inputs the gap widens to 20.52 ms versus 1.25 ms, a factor of 16.4; and on

4.2
Experimental Setup and Functional Ob-
servations
The resource pipeline was exercised against a representa-
tive mix of documents, YouTube videos, images, and web
resources. The pipeline extracted text from uploaded doc-
uments and recursively chunked it according to document
type. Once the chunks were created, two tasks ran in paral-
lel. First, each chunk was embedded and ingested into the
vector store. Second, a representative sample of 5–10% of
the chunks, capped at 30 chunks, was passed to a summa-
rization agent to create a high-level representation of the re-
source. The summary was then embedded and added to the
vector store with resource metadata.

Lt is the top-N LTM slice. The notation ⟨· ∥· ⟩denotes
labelled concatenation: each component is presented as a
distinct, named section of the prompt so that the language
model can treat retrieved resource text, persistent memo-
ries, and conversational context as separate authorities. Each
component is bounded by a tunable budget so that the total
Ct fits within the model’s context window with a config-
urable safety margin.
The Main Agent is the LangGraph node that consumes
Ct, creates structured prompts, and invokes the LLM to gen-
erate the response. When the router selects the council path,
the Main Agent is replaced by the council subgraph; in ev-
ery other case, the Main Agent performs the single model
call. After model invocation, the result is streamed back to
the user, and memory and profile updates are scheduled ac-
cording to the graph workflow.

SCIENTIFIC PRINCIPLES INVOLVED

● ACCELERATION
Since there is acceleration in only in the vertical direction , the velocity in horizontal
direction is constant i.e. ucosθ. But the vertical velocity is changing continuously due to
effect of acceleration due to gravity.
i.e. acceleration in horizontal direction (ax =0)
acceleration in vertical direction (ay =- g)

● VELOCITY OF PROJECTILE AT PERTICULAR INSTANT
Let after time t of projection, the projectile is at a point P (x, y) To calculate
velocity of the projectile at that point P, we have to consider both horizontal and vertical motion.
Resolving velocity u along two axes, at O, we have
Ux= ucos θ and uy = usin θ
If Vx and, are the components of velocity (v) at P in horizontal and vertical directions
respectively, the velocity is given a
V2= Vx2+ Vy2
Where Vx = ux= ucos θ
Vy = usin θ – gt
● Diasplacement
Horizontal displacement = ucos θt
Vertical displacement = usin θ-1/2*gt2

3.4.5
Personalization
Personalization in SaviraNous is a per-topic and per-user
model of understanding. Instead of treating each user as hav-
ing a single scalar “skill level,” the engine maintains topic
profiles, each containing a bounded understanding score
between 0 and 1, evidence, and lists of misconceptions,
strengths, and learning styles. The profiling agent extracts
profiles from conversation segments in structured per-topic
signals. The profile gets updated using EMA (Exponentials
Moving Average) so that a single noisy chat cannot snap the
learner to the extreme estimate. A default is set to cold start

cursor.execute("""
CREATE PROCEDURE insert_student (
IN p_First_Name VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Middle_Name VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Last_Name VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Street VARCHAR(255),
IN p_District VARCHAR(255),
IN p_State VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Country VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Gender ENUM('Male', 'Female', 'Others'),
IN p_Date_of_Birth DATE,
IN p_Email VARCHAR(255),
IN p_College_Email VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Password VARCHAR(255),
IN p_Enrollment_Year YEAR
)
BEGIN
DECLARE CONTINUE HANDLER FOR SQLEXCEPTION
BEGIN
ROLLBACK;
SELECT 'Error: Unable to insert student. Please check the input data.' AS Error;
END;

START TRANSACTION;

INSERT INTO students (
First_Name, Middle_Name, Last_Name, Street, District, State,
Country, Gender, Date_of_Birth, Email, College_Email, Password, Enrollment_Year
) VALUES (
p_First_Name, p_Middle_Name, p_Last_Name, p_Street, p_District, p_State,
p_Country, p_Gender, p_Date_of_Birth, p_Email, p_College_Email, p_Password,
p_Enrollment_Year
);

COMMIT;

2.2
Production sorts and their published analyses
The production sorts of the major language standard libraries are not textbook algorithms; they
are hybrids whose designs have been described in the literature and in the libraries’ own reference
manuals.
Timsort [4] is a hybrid of merge sort and insertion sort that detects already-sorted runs
in the input; it is used by CPython’s sorted and list.sort and by Java’s reference sort for
object arrays. Dual-Pivot Quicksort [5] is a variant of quicksort that selects two pivots per
partition; it is used by Java for primitive arrays. Introsort [6] is a quicksort that monitors its
own recursion depth and switches to heap sort when the depth grows excessive, with insertion
sort used below a small fixed size; it is used by C++’s std::sort and is the production baseline

short-term ENSO forecasts, their skill degrades significantly beyond one season.
Deep learning models have shown promise in extending this predictability hori-

2.3
Machine Learning for Regional Climate Impact Assessment
2.3.1
ENSO-Driven Weather Pattern Prediction
Dutta et al. [5] investigated the application of machine learning for predicting seasonal
weather patterns from ENSO indices, bridging the gap between large-scale ENSO state
prediction and regional climate impacts. Their study employed Random Forest, Gradi-
ent Boosting, and Support Vector Machine classifiers to predict seasonal precipitation
and temperature categories (below normal, normal, above normal) conditioned on mul-
tivariate ENSO indicators including Niño 3.4, Southern Oscillation Index (SOI), and
the Multivariate ENSO Index (MEI).
The results demonstrated that ensemble machine learning methods, particularly XG-
Boost and Random Forest, achieved classification accuracies of 70–82% for seasonal
precipitation categories when ENSO indices from 2–3 months prior were used as pre-
dictors. Feature importance analysis revealed that the combination of Niño 3.4 ampli-

Adaptive-RAG is therefore flexible and helpful for systems
that get different types of queries.
However, adaptive retrieval needs a classifier or judging
mechanism to decide how complex a query is. This adds
system complexity and makes the routing component a key
part of the overall architecture.
The idea of query routing is especially important for Savi-
raNous because SaviraNous uses a Path Judger to pick the
right processing path for a learner query. Thus, SaviraNous
follows an idea of avoiding a single fixed processing path for
every question.

tion
Corrective Retrieval-Augmented Generation (CRAG) takes
the usual retrieval step and adds a corrective part after it [2].
Retrieved documents are evaluated to assess whether they
are good enough to answer the question, and changes are
made before the final answer is generated when they are not.

3.4.1
Active Chat Window
This is the most fine-grained layer, holding the recent mes-
sages of the active conversation (human messages, AI mes-
sages, tool calls, and results). This window is carried on
the LangGraph state and is the layer that makes follow-up
questions answerable: when a learner says, “what is the root
cause behind it?”, the active chat window provides the an-
tecedent of “it”. This active window is intentionally kept
narrow. The content of this window must be passed as it is
without compression or transformation, and it must remain
instantaneously accessible.

2.1
Traditional GOP-Based Scoring
Early pronunciation assessment systems relied heav-
ily on Goodness of Pronunciation (GOP), a
confidence-based scoring method introduced by (Witt
and Young, 2000) for phoneme-level pronuncia-
tion quality. GOP-based systems typically perform
forced alignment between learner speech and canoni-
cal phoneme sequences using acoustic models such as
HMMs or TDNN-F, followed by posterior-probability
estimation to quantify pronunciation correctness.
The official SpeechOcean762 baseline (Zhang et al.,
2021) follows this recipe:
a Kaldi-based GOP
pipeline with phone-specific SVR regressors reaches
phone-level PCC of 0.45. The advantage of GOP-
based methods is that they directly model alignment
and phone posterior confidence; their principal limi-
tation is that they are designed around a single gran-
ularity (phoneme level) and do not provide word- or

5.2
Static-k Evaluation: Standalone-Harness
Run (No Planner)
This section reports the results of running the SaviraNous
retrieval pipeline against four public golden datasets using
the standalone evaluation harness.
The harness reimplements only the retrieval core of the
backend (dense retrieval with optional cross-encoder rerank)
and replaces the backend’s Ollama-based LLM/embedder
with MiniMax-M3 (via the OpenAI Python SDK) and
BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 sentence-transformer em-
beddings.
Backend source files are copied byte-for-byte
where possible; only the chat-model and embedder cou-
plings are rewritten locally.
No planner; fixed-k retriever; no agent decision loop.

• Anomaly Computation
Monthly climatological means are computed for each variable over the 1980–2010
baseline period and subtracted from the raw fields to obtain anomaly fields. This re-
moves the seasonal cycle and isolates interannual variability relevant to ENSO dynam-
ics:
X′(t) = X(t) −¯Xm
(3-3)
where X′(t) is the anomaly at time t, X(t) is the raw value, and ¯Xm is the climatolog-
ical mean for calendar month m.
• Normalization
All anomaly fields are standardized using Z-score normalization computed exclusively
on the training set (1980–2018) to prevent data leakage into the validation and test sets:
ˆX(t) = X′(t) −µtrain
σtrain
(3-4)
where µtrain and σtrain are the mean and standard deviation of the training period anoma-
lies.
• Niño 3.4 Index Computation
The target variable for the El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) forecasting objec-
tive is the Niño 3.4 index, defined as the area-averaged Sea Surface Temperature (SST)

1
INTRODUCTION
Pronunciation assessment plays a critical role in
modern Computer-Assisted Language Learning
(CALL) and Computer-Assisted Pronunciation
Training (CAPT) systems, where automatic evalu-
ation systems provide learners with corrective feed-
back for improving spoken language proficiency (Witt
and Young, 2000; Zhang et al., 2021). Effective lan-
guage learning requires more than an overall pronun-
ciation score: learners need detailed feedback that
identifies articulation errors at the phoneme level, in-
telligibility and stress issues at the word level, and
fluency and prosodic quality at the sentence level.
Such fine-grained feedback enables learners to under-
stand specific weaknesses and improve spoken lan-
guage proficiency more efficiently.
Traditional pronunciation assessment systems
have largely relied on acoustic modelling techniques

4.2.5
Document and Checkpoint Manipulation Experi-
ments
The checkpoint workflow was also exercised for resource-
bearing chat threads. A learner can return to a prior check-
point, restore the session state, and continue asking ques-
tions about the resources available at that point. The learner
can also fork a thread at a prior checkpoint and continue
the conversation as a separate branch after changing the re-
source set, such as adding a new document or revising the
material under discussion.
When a branch is created, the target thread receives the
message prefix, Hippocampus narrative, and PFC state from
the selected checkpoint. The resource-level vector chunks
are copied into the new thread’s FAISS tenant so that re-
trieval remains aligned with the restored resource context.
Source threads remain unchanged. When a checkpoint is
restored in place, the checkpointed graph channels are re-

2.2.2
Autoencoder-LSTM Hybrid Architecture
Dijkstra et al. [4] proposed a hybrid deep learning architecture combining autoencoders
with LSTM networks for ENSO forecasting. Their approach first employs a convolu-
tional autoencoder to compress high-dimensional SST fields into a low-dimensional
latent representation, effectively performing nonlinear dimensionality reduction. The
temporal evolution of these latent features is then modeled using an LSTM network,
which captures the sequential dependencies inherent in ENSO dynamics.
The autoencoder-LSTM framework was trained on SODA reanalysis data and evaluated
against persistence forecasts and linear inverse models. The model demonstrated com-

4.2
Discussion
4.2.1
Findings on each input distribution
The first of the two questions this study set out to answer is addressed by the data above.
• On random inputs the fastest algorithm in this study is std::sort, followed within
run-to-run noise by the hand-rolled random-pivot quicksort, followed at a distance by heap
sort. The three quadratic algorithms are unusable past a few thousand elements: their
running time at 𝑁= 105 is measured in seconds to tens of seconds, while the linearithmic
algorithms remain in the millisecond regime.

The phoneme-level result (0.396 PCC) substantially

2.3
FlashRAG
FlashRAG presents a framework for retrieval-augmented
generation research [3]. Its architecture separates the stages
of the RAG pipeline—judging, retrieval, reranking, refine-
ment, and generation— into modular components that can
be replaced or experimented with independently. This gives
flexibility for researchers and developers who wish to eval-
uate retrieval or generation strategies.
One limitation of FlashRAG is that it is primarily a frame-
work rather than a single retrieval algorithm, so its effective-
ness depends on the components chosen for the pipeline.
The modular design of FlashRAG is relevant to Savi-
raNous because SaviraNous’s architecture also separates

3.1
Overall System Architecture
The SaviraNous engine is decomposed into 4 cooperating
layers at the system level:
i. User Interface layer containing the shipped sandboxed
Electron desktop client and a Manifest V3 browser side
panel included in the repository as an unpacked exten-
sion that can be installed through the browser’s devel-
oper mode. This layer communicates directly with the
FastAPI backend, and both components are indepen-
dently deployable. They communicate with the back-
end through authenticated streaming endpoints.
ii. Backend layer implemented with FastAPI and Pydantic
exposes typed HTTP/SSE APIs for chat, profile man-
agement, authentication and administration endpoints.

provide substantial performance benefits over straightforward textbook implementations.

algorithmic complexity and real-world sorting performance.

5.4
Generalisation Across Speaker
Groups
The speaker-group analysis reveals a persistent per-
formance gap between adult and child speech across
all evaluation tasks. For example, sentence-fluency
prediction reaches 0.835 PCC for adult speech but
only 0.567 for child speech.
This discrepancy
likely results from greater acoustic variability in child
speech:
inconsistent articulation patterns, higher
pitch variation, unstable speaking rates, irregular
pause behaviour, and greater pronunciation variabil-
ity.

4. Queries
These are some queries used in this project :
4.1 Drop database
4.1.1 code
def drop_database():
"""Drops the specified database if it exists."""
connection = None
cursor = None
try:
# Connect without specifying a database initially to run DROP DATABASE
connection = get_connection()
if connection is None:
return # Connection failed, already printed error

cursor = connection.cursor()
# Use backticks around the database name in SQL
print(f"\nDropping database `{DATABASE_NAME}` if it exists...")
cursor.execute(f"DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS `{DATABASE_NAME}`;")
connection.commit()
print(f"Database `{DATABASE_NAME}` dropped successfully.")
except pymysql.Error as err:
print(f"Error dropping database: {err}", file=sys.stderr)
finally:
if cursor:
cursor.close()
if connection:
connection.close()

Integer comparison cost.
All seven textbook algorithms compare integers. C++ std::sort
also compares integers in this benchmark. On string keys, on double keys, or on key types
where comparison invokes a virtual method, the comparison cost dominates and the wall-clock
behaviour changes. The qualitative conclusions survive, but the constant factors do not transfer.
Future research should extend the benchmark to string keys, doubles, and comparator-object
keys.
Black-box view of std::sort.
The most important caveat specific to this study is that
std::sort is treated as a black box: its comparisons and writes are not observable. The
wall-clock numbers are first-class measurements, but the comparison and write counts in the
plots and tables are not directly comparable with the textbook algorithms. Inferences about why

1.4
Proposed Solution: SaviraNous
To overcome these interconnected challenges, we propose
SaviraNous, an integrated, local-first intelligent tutoring en-
gine designed to execute on consumer-grade hardware with
at least 8 GB of RAM. SaviraNous unifies conversational
memory, dynamic retrieval, query routing, and multi-agent
deliberation into a stateful graph-orchestrated framework
(implemented via LangChain’s ecosystem, FastAPI, and lo-
cal Ollama runtimes). The architecture directly resolves the
four identified research gaps through four core mechanisms:
1. Persistent Cognitive User Modeling (Resolving Gap
1): Instead of starting from scratch every time, Savi-
raNous uses a maintained user’s profile. In the back-
ground, it continuously tracks how well a user under-
stands each topic using an Exponential Moving Aver-
age (EMA, α = 0.7). By separating user progress from
temporary chat sessions, the system remembers mas-

1.4
Research Questions
The research questions addressed by this study are:
1. What is the current ranking of the seven textbook sorting algorithms on random, sorted,
and reverse-sorted inputs at 𝑁= 105?
2. What factors — input distribution, value range, cache behaviour, write count — influence
the empirical performance of each algorithm?
3. How does C++ std::sort (Introsort) compare to the textbook merge sort and quicksort
on identical inputs?
4. What recommendations can be developed from the findings for practitioners choosing
between textbook and production sort implementations?
1.5
Significance of the Study
This study matters to several groups. Students learning algorithms gain concrete, quantitative
material that goes beyond asymptotic tables and illustrates how input distribution and constant
factors shift the empirical ordering of algorithms. Educators gain a reference data set for

INTRODUCTION
Any object thrown into atmosphere so that it falls under the effect of gravity alone is called
projectile. Path of the projectile is called its trajectory For projectile motion, air resistance is
neglected and acceleration due to gravity is downward and constant. A stone thrown
horizontally, a bomb dropped from an airplane and any object dropped from the window of a
moving train, etc. are examples of projectile.
When a projectile is moving in the air, it has horizontal and vertical velocities. The horizontal
velocity remains constant throughout its motion whereas its vertical motion is affected by
gravity. So, the trajectory of the projectile is determined by the combined velocity due to the
gravity and the horizontal velocity of the projectile. Motion of the projectile takes place on a
plane i.e. two-dimensional motion

MemoRAG focuses on memory-
guided retrieval.
With these advances, each method brings its own trade-
offs. Graph- and tree-based methods need preprocessing and
infrastructure. Memory-enhanced methods depend on the
quality of stored or generated memory. Corrective and self-
reflective methods need model operations. Adaptive meth-
ods need routing or judging mechanisms.
For a learning system, retrieval alone is not enough. A
learner might ask factual questions, questions about up-
loaded learning resources, or follow-up questions that de-
pend on earlier interactions. These query types require dif-
ferent processing paths and different kinds of context.
Therefore, a learning-oriented architecture is needed that
combines query processing, relevant resource selection, se-
mantic retrieval, memory management, and response refine-
ment, so that simpler queries can avoid the most computa-
tionally expensive retrieval and council stages.

3.4
Cross-Attention Spoken–Reference
Alignment
To compare spoken pronunciation against expected
pronunciation structure, the model applies cross-
attention between acoustic speech states and canon-
ical reference representations. The attention mecha-
nism follows the standard Transformer attention for-
mulation introduced by (Vaswani et al., 2017):
Attention(Q,K,V) = Softmax
(︃QK⊤
√dk
)︃
V,
(4)
where Q = Ha (acoustic speech representation), K =
V = P (canonical phoneme representation).
The
aligned representation becomes
Halign = Attention(Ha,P,P).
(5)
This mechanism enables the model to learn de-
viations between expected and observed pronuncia-
tion. The output is added back to the acoustic stream
through a residual connection, and a layer normali-
sation is applied before downstream hierarchical pro-
cessing.

3.5
Hierarchical Representation
Learning
Pronunciation quality naturally follows hierarchical
linguistic structure. HiPPA first predicts phoneme-
level pronunciation quality from the aligned acoustic
stream, then aggregates phoneme representations into
word representations, and finally aggregates word
representations into sentence representations.
Phoneme representations are aggregated to con-
struct word-level representations:
Wj = Aggregateword
(︁
{Pi}i∈word j
)︁
,
(6)
whereWj denotes the word representation constructed
from the constituent phonemes of word j. The aggre-
gation uses attention pooling with a learnable query,
similar to the word-attention pooling used in (Gong
et al., 2022; Chen et al., 2024).
Word representations are further aggregated to
construct sentence-level representations:
S = Aggregatesent

where m is the total number of words in the sen-

4.1.5
Comparisons and writes
The benchmark records the number of comparisons and the number of writes performed by
each textbook algorithm. The corresponding counts for std::sort are not observable and are
reported as zero.
The comparison count for insertion sort on already-sorted input is exactly 𝑁−1: every
element is compared with its left neighbour and the loop then exits. The comparison count for
selection sort on already-sorted input is exactly 𝑁(𝑁−1)
2
: it does not save work even when the
work is unnecessary. The comparison count for quicksort with random pivot is approximately
1.4𝑁log2 𝑁on random data; on sorted input it is a comparable 1.3𝑁log2 𝑁thanks to the random

• On already-sorted inputs the fastest algorithm is insertion sort, with bubble sort a close
second thanks to its early-termination test. std::sort is third but is the fastest of the
𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛)-class algorithms by a factor of approximately 5 over quicksort and 16 over
merge sort. Selection sort is the only quadratic algorithm that does not benefit from the
input being sorted, and on already-sorted inputs at 𝑁= 105 it is approximately 8.7 × 104
times slower than insertion sort.
• On reverse-sorted inputs the fastest algorithm is counting sort, with std::sort a close
second and quicksort third. Merge sort and heap sort follow at a factor of approximately
15 over std::sort. Insertion sort is the worst-case baseline for this input.
The structural reason for these results is the design of the algorithms themselves (Section 3):
the three quadratic algorithms have a content-agnostic inner loop (selection sort) or a content-

4.2.4
End-to-End Integration Experiments
End-to-end integration was verified by running representa-
tive learner sessions that exercised the profile loading for
the cold start user, a sequence of turns that progressively
grew the chat window and triggered the Hippocampus sum-
marization, an explicit council invocation, a resource upload
followed by resource-grounded questions, and an idle period
during which the profile extraction stage was invoked. In all
cases, the integrated workflow produced the expected arti-
fact at each stage: a profile was visible in the prompt, the
Hippocampus narrative shortened after summarization, the
council returned a structured report, and resource-grounded
answers reflected the uploaded material.

CNN independently and identically across all 12 time steps yields a sequence of spatial
feature vectors:
F = [f(1), f(2), . . . , f(12)] ∈R12×d
(3-10)
where each vector f(t) summarises the spatial state of the tropical Pacific at month t.
Stage 2 : Temporal Encoder (TCN): The sequence of spatial feature vectors F is
passed into a Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN) consisting of stacked dilated
causal convolutional layers that model the temporal evolution of the extracted spatial
patterns across the 12-month input window. Causal convolutions ensure that the repre-
sentation at any time step is conditioned only on past and present observations, prevent-
ing information leakage from future time steps. Dilation is applied with exponentially
increasing rates across successive layers:
dl = 2l−1,
l = 1, 2, 3, . . . , L
(3-11)
where dl is the dilation rate at layer l and L is the total number of dilated convolutional

At the storage layer, PostgreSQL holds the persistent ap-
plication data (users, threads, messages, profiles, quizzes)
and pgvector backend LTM. Redis is used for transient state
such as rate limiting and active chat thread pointer. FAISS
provides in-memory per-session chunk indices that are rehy-
drated when a session is resumed through the checkpointer.
Latency management is treated as a first-class concern
of the orchestration layer rather than an afterthought to the
LLM call. The conversational graph is executed on a ded-
icated worker pool, and the HTTP route polls the check-
pointer so that the response can be returned once the AI
message is available. The Hippocampus writer and LTM
extraction stage may continue after response delivery. This

ˆyENSO = fENSO(z) ∈R

The third,

existing hand-curated Q&A seed set by rewriting the seed
input and expected output into harder variants (paraphrase,

hand-curated dataset.

4.2.3
Why standard libraries don’t ship plain quicksort
The textbook quicksort has its own three problems.
1. It has an 𝑂(𝑛2) worst case on adversarial inputs. The classical deterministic-pivot variant
(first element or last element as pivot) is degenerate on sorted and reverse-sorted inputs; a
malicious input specifically crafted against the algorithm can force quadratic time even
against random-pivot variants if the random-number generator is predictable.
2. It is not stable. A sort is stable when it preserves the relative order of equal-keyed
elements. Quicksort, in any common implementation, is not. Stability is required or
strongly preferred in a number of common scenarios — sort-by-name then sort-by-grade,
sort a database table by an indexed column then by an unindexed one — and a non-stable
sort forces the caller to add a tiebreaker manually.
3. It is recursive, and on adversarial inputs the recursion can be very deep. Production sorts

4. EXPECTED OUTCOMES
Upon completion, this project is expected to deliver the following outcomes:
1. Functional Deep Learning Pipeline: A complete Python-based forecasting sys-
tem capable of ingesting reanalysis data and producing ENSO state predictions at
6 month lead times, made available as an open-source GitHub repository.
2. ENSO Forecast Performance: The CNN-TCN model aims to achieve competi-
tive forecasting skill relative to baseline machine learning approaches.
3. June-July-August-September 2026 Precipitation and Temperature Anomaly
Forecasts: Forecasts of June-July-August-September 2026 precipitation and tem-
perature anomalies over South Asia conditioned on the predicted El Niño state.
4. Comparative Analysis: A systematic comparison of the proposed CNN-TCN
approach against baseline method (XGBoost) and existing deep learning archi-
tectures, demonstrating the value of the chosen architecture and multi-task learn-
ing.

non-null, the function records every comparison and every element move or swap it performs.
The counters are zero-cost when the struct is null, so the same code powers both a minimal demo
and the instrumented benchmark. C++ std::sort was added to the same harness through a
wrapper that conforms to the C function-pointer signature; its comparisons and writes are not
visible to the harness, so the corresponding counter cells in the benchmark output are reported
as zero.
To verify that the added increments do not distort the timing, an alternative benchmark with
the counters disabled was run on a subset of the cells; the wall-clock times agreed with the
instrumented times to within run-to-run noise. The counters are therefore not corrected for in
what follows.

3.6
Auxiliary CTC-Based
Pronunciation Confidence
Modelling
To improve low-level phoneme discrimination, the
architecture incorporates an auxiliary Connection-
ist Temporal Classification (CTC) branch (Graves
et al., 2006).
The CTC head predicts canonical
phoneme sequences directly from acoustic states:
Pctc = Softmax(WctcHa +b).
(8)
From this branch the model extracts pronuncia-
tion confidence features that are concatenated with the
phoneme representation Pi before the phone regres-
sion head:
• mean canonical phoneme log-probability,
• maximum canonical phoneme log-probability,
• top-2 competitor margin,
• relative temporal duration.

practice. The cost of a cache miss is roughly two orders of magnitude larger than the cost of
an arithmetic operation, so the cache behaviour of an algorithm can dominate its wall-clock
time even within the same Big-O class; merge sort, for example, makes roughly five times as
many writes as quicksort on random data (≈2𝑁log2 𝑁versus ≈0.4𝑁log2 𝑁). Real-world data
is rarely uniformly random, so a sort that ignores input shape leaves performance on the table;
this is the design intuition behind Timsort’s run detection [4] and pdqsort’s adversarial-pattern
detection [7]. The literature therefore converges on hybridisation as the production answer: the
textbook sorts are the building blocks, not the alternatives, used in production.

3.4.7
Validation and Evaluation
A chronological train–validate–test split is employed to evaluate model performance,
ensuring that no future information is accessible during training or validation:
• Training: 1980–2018
• Validation: 2019–2022
• Testing: 2023–2025
Hyperparameters for both the Convolutional Neural Network – Temporal Convolutional
Network (CNN-TCN) and the XGBoost baseline are tuned exclusively on the validation
set. Final performance for both models is reported on the held-out test set (2023–2025).

in bytes that the algorithm holds at any moment during the run. As noted above, these three
quantities are not observable for C++ std::sort.
The benchmark was compiled with gcc on a Windows 11 workstation and linked against the
C++ standard library for the std::sort wrapper. No other workloads were running during the
timed runs.
Three implementation choices matter for the discussion in Section 4.2:
1. Quicksort uses a uniformly random pivot. The classical textbook version, which selects the
first or last element as the pivot, degenerates to quadratic time on already-sorted input. The
implementation in this study does not, and the resulting quicksort timings on sorted and
reverse-sorted inputs are therefore faster than a naive textbook quicksort would produce.
2. Merge sort allocates two auxiliary buffers per recursive merge call. No attempt is made to
reuse a single shared buffer at the top level, so the memory figure reported for merge sort

𝑁= 105, merge sort takes 24.99 milliseconds and std::sort takes 9.92 milliseconds;
on sorted inputs the gap widens to 20.52 ms versus 1.25 ms, a factor of 16.4; and on

cache-bound hardware.
3. It does not detect already-sorted runs in the input. Real data (transaction logs, sorted-name
dictionaries, time series) has such runs frequently, and merge sort pays the full 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛)
cost on them rather than treating them as already-sorted sub-problems.
The first point generalises: any merge-sort-family algorithm that relies on a single top-level
buffer pays this memory cost. The Timsort used by Python and Java [4] pays the same cost
in exchange for stability and for the ability to skip already-sorted runs; it does not pay the

Project Overview
The proposed capstone project focuses on the design and implementation of a
local-first, multi-agent conversational AI tutor system aimed at delivering
personalized learning experiences. Unlike traditional AI tutors that rely heavily on cloud-
based, generic responses, this system emphasizes user privacy, adaptability, and
contextual awareness. It integrates advanced components such as hybrid Retrieval-
Augmented Generation (RAG), adaptive memory strategies, and multimodal interaction
to create a more intelligent and responsive tutoring environment. The system is designed
to support students across multiple subjects while maintaining flexibility in deployment
and control over data.
At its core, the system employs modular layered architecture consisting of a user
interface layer, a FastAPI backend, and an AI council orchestrator that coordinates
multiple specialized agents. These agents collaboratively handle tasks such as concept

constant multiple of log2 𝑛, introsort switches to heap sort for the remaining work. Below
a small fixed size (typically sixteen elements) it uses insertion sort. The hybrid guarantees
𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) worst case and achieves close-to-quicksort average performance.
In our
measurements, std::sort sits within run-to-run noise of the hand-rolled random-pivot
quicksort on random inputs, and beats every other algorithm on sorted and reverse-sorted
inputs except insertion sort on sorted.
• CPython (sorted and list.sort) uses Timsort, the algorithm of [4]. Timsort is a
hybrid: it walks the input to detect naturally sorted runs, sorts short runs with insertion
sort, and merges runs with a merge-sort-style merge. Timsort is stable.
• Java (Arrays.sort for objects) uses Timsort [4] for the same reason — stability matters
in object sorts.

8. Students and Fees:
•
Total Participation (Fee) & Partial Participation (Student): Every fee must be associated
with a student, but a student may not have paid any fees. Thus, fees participate totally, while
students participate partially.
9. Admin Management:
•

cannot exist without management authority. Therefore, both participate totally.
10. Students and Results:
•
Total Participation (Result) & Partial Participation (Student): Every result is linked to a
student, but not all students may have results published. Thus, results participate totally, while
students participate partially.

ized query, using cross-attention over the query and docu-
ments. The reranker emits a relevance score for each docu-

to prevent context pollution before context creation. This
is done because sentence level extraction inside the docu-
ments is expected to absorb any remaining noise. This mod-

3.3.5
Context Construction
Once the documents and memories have been reranked, they
are aggregated into a context. This aggregation is bounded,
labelled, and explicitly partitioned so that the downstream
model treats each context region as a distinct authority.
3.4
Hierarchical Memory Architecture
The central design decision of the SaviraNous engine is that
learner context is not stored as a single conversation history.
Instead, the context is layered into four cooperating repre-
sentations, each having a distinct lifecycle, granularity and
role.

5.3.1
System under test
The RAG system evaluated here is a LangGraph-based agent
that combines dense vector retrieval with a cross-encoder
reranker, an LLM-conditioned response synthesiser, and a
Postgres-backed check-pointer.
Table 9 lists the compo-
nents; only model names are exposed — no corpus, docu-
ment titles, or chunk contents are reported.
The judge model is deliberately a different model from the
generator (M2.7 vs M3) to mitigate judge-bias — a known
failure mode where a model scoring its own outputs inflates
the metrics.

3. It is recursive, and on adversarial inputs the recursion can be very deep. Production sorts

4.2.4
What the libraries actually ship
The production sorts of the major languages are constructed so as to retain the strengths of merge
sort and quicksort while patching the failures of each. The empirical row that this study adds is
the std::sort row: it confirms that the gap between the textbook algorithm and the production
baseline is real and quantifiable.
• C++ std::sort uses Introsort [6], the algorithm measured directly in this study.
Introsort is a quicksort that monitors its own recursion depth; when the depth exceeds a
constant multiple of log2 𝑛, introsort switches to heap sort for the remaining work. Below
a small fixed size (typically sixteen elements) it uses insertion sort. The hybrid guarantees

Selection Criterion: Features are selected iteratively in descending order of their
redundancy-penalized score S(Xi,j) until a predetermined number of features K is
retained. The value of K is determined through cross-validation on the validation set
(2019–2022) by evaluating XGBoost performance across a range of feature set sizes and
selecting the value of K that minimises validation Root Mean Square Error (RMSE).
Application: The MIFS procedure is applied exclusively to the XGBoost baseline
model. The selected K features are extracted from the flattened one-dimensional in-
put vector and fed directly into XGBoost for training and inference. The CNN-TCN
model receives the full unfiltered input tensor of shape (12×30×100×4) without any
prior feature selection, as the spatial encoder handles all feature extraction implicitly

1. INTRODUCTION
1.1
Background
The El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) is the dominant mode of interannual climate
variability, influencing weather patterns worldwide through coupled ocean-atmosphere
interactions in the tropical Pacific [1]. ENSO cycles between warm (El Niño) and cool
(La Niña) phases, driven primarily by anomalies in Sea Surface Temperature (SST)
across the equatorial Pacific Ocean. These events have far-reaching teleconnections
that affect precipitation, temperature, and extreme weather events across multiple con-
tinents.
South Asia, where the Indian Summer Monsoon provides over 70% of annual rain-
fall, is highly vulnerable to ENSO-induced climate anomalies [2]. El Niño events are
associated with weakened monsoon circulation, leading to droughts in the Indian sub-
continent, while La Niña events often intensify monsoon rainfall [3]. Climate outlooks
indicate the possibility of ENSO-related anomalies during 2026, highlighting the im-

• Java (Arrays.sort for objects) uses Timsort [4] for the same reason — stability matters
in object sorts.
• Java (Arrays.sort for primitive arrays) uses a dual-pivot quicksort [5] that selects two
pivots per partition. Stability is not required for primitives, so the dual-pivot variant’s
somewhat faster average-case running time is preferred over Timsort.
• Go’s sorting implementation uses pattern-defeating quicksort (pdqsort) [7, 9], a
quicksort that detects adversarial partition patterns and switches to heap sort when one is
observed. (The implementation lives in the sort package and was adopted in Go 1.19;
the generic slices package adopted it in Go 1.21.)

sorts, 𝑂(𝑛log 𝑛) for the divide-and-conquer and heap-based sorts, and 𝑂(𝑛+ 𝑘) for counting

Therefore, this study focuses on an empirical benchmark of seven textbook sorts and C++
std::sort across three input distributions.
1.2
Statement of the Problem
Despite the importance of sorting algorithms in practice, there is limited understanding of how
the textbook asymptotic classification translates into wall-clock performance on real hardware
and across different input distributions. As a consequence, practitioners may select algorithms

2.4
GraphRAG
GraphRAG introduces a way to use a graph to help with re-
trieving and generating information. In this method, details
from documents are shown using a knowledge graph and
summaries of groups [4]. The method is made to help with
questions that need to understand how different things are
connected.
A big benefit of GraphRAG is that it can help with think-
ing about parts at once. Instead of just finding separate parts,
the graph can link related parts and help with thinking across
many documents.
Making and keeping the graph takes a lot of work. Tak-
ing out the parts, building the links, finding groups, making
summaries and then thinking about it all can take computer
power and more time. So this method can get very costly
when there are a lot of documents or when the information

For complex queries that warrant deeper investigation,

This design aims to give a foundation for context-aware
and personalized learning interactions.

Objectives
The primary objectives of this capstone project are as follows:
1. Design a Local-First Architecture
To develop a conversational AI system that prioritizes user data privacy and control by
enabling local execution, while also supporting optional cloud integration for enhanced
computational capabilities.
2. Implement a Multi-Agent AI Council
To design and implement a multi-agent system consisting of specialized AI agents (such
as tutor, evaluation, memory, and privacy agents) that collaboratively improve
reasoning, response quality, and personalized tutoring.
3. Develop a Hybrid RAG Pipeline
To build an advanced retrieval-augmented generation system that combines multiple
retrieval techniques, including vector-based search, Self-RAG evaluation, and Corrective
RAG filtering, to enhance response accuracy and contextual relevance.
4. Integrate Student Evaluation and Learning Analytics
To create assessment tools such as quizzes, flashcards, and performance dashboards

RELATED WORK
Automatic pronunciation assessment has been an
active research area within speech processing and
language-learning systems, particularly in the domain
of CAPT. The primary objective of pronunciation as-
sessment systems is to evaluate the quality of learner
speech and provide corrective feedback that improves
language acquisition. Over the years, research in this
area has evolved from traditional statistical acoustic-
modelling approaches towards modern deep-learning
and transformer-based architectures.
2.1
Traditional GOP-Based Scoring
Early pronunciation assessment systems relied heav-
ily on Goodness of Pronunciation (GOP), a
confidence-based scoring method introduced by (Witt

When k was raised to compensate, recall recovered but
the additional candidates dragged relevance down.
Pre-
cision@10 collapsed, irrelevant context leaked into the
prompt, and the response synthesiser began grounding its
answers in noise. The full per-metric breakdown in Table 7
shows the trade-off directly: precision falls monotonically
as k grows from 1 to 100, while recall rises monotonically;
the two curves cross but neither reaches a good operating
point at any single fixed k.
At large k the harness pays
a triple cost: context pollution — chunks the model reads
but cannot use, dragging relevance down; token wastage —
every additional chunk consumes a non-trivial share of the
model’s context window, inflating per-turn prompt size, in-
ference latency, and dollar cost; and downstream generation

Experimental Setup
Experiments
are
conducted
using
the
Spee-
chOcean762
benchmark
(Zhang
et
al.,
2021)
consisting of 2,500 English training utterances and
2,500 test utterances from 250 Mandarin-speaking
English learners.
The dataset provides expert
pronunciation annotations at phoneme, word, and
sentence levels. The architecture uses a pretrained
WavLM-Base-Plus encoder (Chen et al., 2022) as
the acoustic backbone, with the last two transformer
layers unfrozen during training.

Content:
1. NotebookLM – Google
NotebookLM is an AI-powered research assistant developed by Google that helps
users understand and organize information from their own documents. Users can upload
materials such as PDFs, research papers, or notes, and the system generates summaries and
explanations based on the uploaded content. This allows students to explore their study
materials more efficiently.
The system uses a technique called Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG),
where relevant information is first retrieved from the uploaded documents and then used
by the AI model to generate responses. This approach helps improve the accuracy of
explanations because the AI refers directly to the user’s documents instead of relying only
on general knowledge.
Advantages
•
Works directly with uploaded documents and study materials.
•
Helps summarize and analyze large amounts of information.

Need Analysis
The rapid integration of Artificial Intelligence in education has led to the
emergence of conversational tutoring systems; however, most existing solutions remain
generic, cloud-dependent, and minimally adaptive to individual learners. Current
platforms typically provide one-size-fits-all responses without considering a student’s
personal learning materials, pace, or weak areas. Additionally, these systems often treat
each interaction independently, lacking mechanisms for contextual continuity and long-
term learning progression. This creates a gap between human tutoring—where
personalization and adaptive teaching are central—and AI-based tutoring systems, which
still struggle to replicate such depth and contextual awareness.
Another critical issue is the over-reliance on cloud infrastructure, which raises
concerns related to privacy, latency, and accessibility. Many students, especially in

Figure 1: Overall architecture of the HiPPA framework. The WavLM acoustic encoder produces a stack of hidden states; a
learnable layer-weighted combination yields the acoustic stream. Reference-conditioned phoneme embeddings (phoneme +
position + handcrafted phonetic features) are combined with the acoustic stream via cross-attention. Phoneme representations
are aggregated into word representations and then into a sentence representation carrying additional global prosodic features;
the CTC branch shares the WavLM output and feeds auxiliary confidence features to the phone head. The phone, word, and
sentence regression heads are trained jointly.

2.2.4 Relationship Analysis (Participation)
The participation of entities in various relationships within the University Management System is
described below:
1. Students and Courses:
•
Partial Participation (Both) A student may not be enrolled in any course, and some courses
may have no students enrolled. Therefore, both entities participate partially in the relationship.
2. Faculty and Department:
•
Total Participation (Faculty) & Partial Participation (Department): Every faculty member
must belong to a department, but a department can exist without any faculty members. Faculty
participation is total, while department participation is partial.
3. Faculty and Courses:
•
Total Participation (Faculty) & Partial Participation (Course): Every faculty member must
teach at least one course. However, some courses may not be assigned to any faculty. Therefore,
faculty participation is total, while course participation is partial.

3.4.4
Feature Selection
Given the high dimensionality of the flattened input (12 × 30 × 100 × 4 = 144,000
features per sample), a feature selection step is applied prior to training the XGBoost
baseline model to identify and retain only those spatial grid cells that carry statistically
significant predictive information for El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) forecast-
ing. Mutual Information Feature Selection (MIFS) is employed as the feature selec-
tion criterion, operating directly on the continuous Niño 3.4 index values as the target
variable. This approach preserves the full information content of the target without

The conclusions above depend on assumptions that are not fully satisfied by the measurements
in this paper.
Architecture dependence.
The wall-clock numbers are specific to the workstation on which
the benchmark was run, and the relative ordering of algorithms within a single asymptotic class
— notably the merge sort versus quicksort versus heap sort ordering on random data — is affected
by the cache sizes of the CPU. The qualitative ordering (which algorithm class is faster) and the
gap between std::sort and the textbook algorithms are robust; the precise ratios should be
re-measured on the target architecture before committing to a specific algorithm in production.
Single-input distribution.
The benchmark covers only three of the many possible input
distributions. Inputs that are nearly sorted but not perfectly sorted, inputs that are sorted in
blocks, inputs with a small number of distinct keys — all of these are common in practice and all

3.2
Query Processing and Path Selection
The vision of the SaviraNous engine is that every query does
not require the same expensive path. A query may be a di-
rect factual clarification that can be answered with paramet-
ric knowledge, a knowledge-grounded question that requires
ingested resources, or an investigative request. Routing ev-
ery query through the same expensive path would introduce
unnecessary retrieval, context pollution, and inference cost.
To prevent this, the engine uses a Path Judger.
The Path Judger is a dedicated classifier whose role is to
emit, for each turn, a single labelled path choice that iden-
tifies the processing strategy and any resources required by
downstream stages. It selects from a defined set of paths
using compressed context, the active chat window, the re-
source profile, and the current query. Together, these inputs
form a contextualized query that is sent to the router. The

==========================================================================================
Point ID:    f8fa98cd-1610-59a4-84a7-7988fcb2c06f
Document ID: 6c0b6913-dcb6-49d4-a994-f56c99c7b7ae
Chunk index: 23
Source:      SaviraNous.pdf
Page:        4
Metadata:    {'producer': 'pdfTeX-1.40.29', 'creator': 'TeX', 'creationdate': '2026-10-18T13:27:40+00:00', 'source': 'SaviraNous.pdf', 'file_path': 'C:\\Users\\miyas\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\voice_ai_indexing_o34iej6k\\SaviraNous.pdf', 'total_pages': 23, 'format': 'PDF 1.5', 'title': '', 'author': '', 'subject': '', 'keywords': '', 'moddate': '2026-10-18T13:27:40+00:00', 'trapped': '', 'modDate': 'D:20261018132740Z', 'creationDate': 'D:20261018132740Z', 'page': 4, 'extraction_method': 'pymupdf', 'native_text_length': 4538, 'ocr_attempted': False, 'ocr_used': False, 'start_index': 1592, 'organization_id': '3976ac6b-fd4e-4b44-beda-6c516b45a245', 'document_id': '6c0b6913-dcb6-49d4-a994-f56c99c7b7ae', 'document_version_id': '2d7e4568-f658-44c6-a75b-f04f966d462f', 'version_number': 1, 'chunk_index': 23, 'status': 'active'}
Content:
especially useful for long-term and multi-hop information
retrieval.
HippoRAG provides a mechanism for retrieving informa-
tion and for linking evidence spread across a knowledge col-
lection. Building the graph and maintaining the retrieval
pipeline adds additional complexity, and parameters must be
tuned carefully for the approach to be effective.
SaviraNous tackles the same problem from a different
architectural angle.
Rather than running the whole Hip-
poRAG pipeline, SaviraNous splits conversational data, cor-
pus information, and long-term data into separate memory-
focused parts: the Active Chat Window holds recent turns,
the Hippocampus compresses older turns, the Prefrontal
Cortex (PFC) tracks resource-level summaries, and Long-
Term Memory (LTM) persists facts across sessions. These
parts let the system look at past context when it builds a re-
sponse.
2.6
LightRAG
LightRAG aims to make retrieval-augmented generation

pronunciation assessment systems. Specialised scor-

MuFFIN (Yan et al., 2025) remain highly effective
for isolated phoneme-level evaluation because of ex-

The next parts look at nine approaches that relate to build-
ing SaviraNous. The papers cover Adaptive-RAG, Correc-
tive RAG, FlashRAG, GraphRAG, HippoRAG, LightRAG,
MemoRAG, RAPTOR and Self-RAG.
2.1
Adaptive-RAG
Adaptive-RAG brings a way where the retrieval strategy is
chosen based on how complex the user’s question is [1].
Rather than sending every query through the same retrieval
pipeline, Adaptive-RAG decides if a query is simple enough
to answer right away or if it needs more retrieval and reason-
ing.
The main benefit of this approach is that retrieval can be
skipped when it is not needed. This can lower overhead and
let more complex questions get a better retrieval strategy.
Adaptive-RAG is therefore flexible and helpful for systems
that get different types of queries.
However, adaptive retrieval needs a classifier or judging

run through the production SaviraNous LangGraph engine:
the planner directs every query to retrieve only by
context, the retriever agent performs dense retrieval with a
cross-encoder rerank, the response synthesiser produces the
final answer, and the Postgres check-pointer persists each
turn.
DeepEval contributes only the judge LLM and the
metric runner that scores the engine’s output. Because the
engine’s full pipeline is exercised, this run does not break
the engine.
Together these give a two-axis view: does the retrieval
core behave correctly on large public benchmarks, with the
engine stripped down to FAISS + rerank? (static-k), and
does the system answer well end-to-end on a small cu-
rated set, going through the planner-driven live engine?
(dynamic-k). The motivation for maintaining both is given
in Section 5.1, and a short synthesis is given in Section 5.4.
